## 3.9 Complete Step-by-Step Solutions to Chapter Exercises

### Solution to Exercise 3.1
Evaluate $\lim_{x \to 5} (3x^2 - 4x + 7)$.
Since the expression is a polynomial, it is continuous everywhere. Substitute $x = 5$ directly:
$$\lim_{x \to 5} (3x^2 - 4x + 7) = 3(5)^2 - 4(5) + 7 = 3(25) - 20 + 7 = 75 - 20 + 7 = 62$$
**Final Answer:** $62$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 3.2
Evaluate $\lim_{x \to -2} \frac{x^2 - 4}{x + 2}$.
1. Direct substitution gives $\frac{(-2)^2 - 4}{-2 + 2} = \frac{0}{0}$.
2. Factor the numerator: $x^2 - 4 = (x - 2)(x + 2)$.
3. Cancel the common factor $(x + 2)$ for $x \neq -2$:
$$\lim_{x \to -2} \frac{(x - 2)(x + 2)}{x + 2} = \lim_{x \to -2} (x - 2)$$
4. Substitute $x = -2$:
$$-2 - 2 = -4$$
**Final Answer:** $-4$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 3.3
For $f(x) = 5x - 8$, evaluate $\lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(3+h) - f(3)}{h}$.
1. Compute $f(3) = 5(3) - 8 = 15 - 8 = 7$.
2. Compute $f(3+h) = 5(3+h) - 8 = 15 + 5h - 8 = 7 + 5h$.
3. Form the numerator: $f(3+h) - f(3) = (7 + 5h) - 7 = 5h$.
4. Divide by $h$: $\frac{5h}{h} = 5$ for $h \neq 0$.
5. Take the limit: $\lim_{h \to 0} 5 = 5$.
**Final Answer:** $5$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 3.4
For $f(x) = -2x^2 + 6x$, compute the rate of change at $x = 1$.
1. Compute $f(1) = -2(1)^2 + 6(1) = -2 + 6 = 4$.
2. Compute $f(1+h)$:
$$f(1+h) = -2(1+h)^2 + 6(1+h) = -2(1 + 2h + h^2) + 6 + 6h$$
$$f(1+h) = -2 - 4h - 2h^2 + 6 + 6h = 4 + 2h - 2h^2$$
3. Compute the difference: $f(1+h) - f(1) = (4 + 2h - 2h^2) - 4 = 2h - 2h^2$.
4. Divide by $h$: $\frac{2h - 2h^2}{h} = 2 - 2h$.
5. Evaluate the limit as $h \to 0$:
$$\lim_{h \to 0} (2 - 2h) = 2 - 2(0) = 2$$
**Final Answer:** $2$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 3.5
Evaluate $\lim_{u \to 0} \frac{\sqrt{4 + u} - 2}{u}$.
1. At $u = 0$, the expression gives $\frac{\sqrt{4} - 2}{0} = \frac{0}{0}$.
2. Rationalize by multiplying numerator and denominator by $(\sqrt{4 + u} + 2)$:
$$\frac{(\sqrt{4 + u} - 2)(\sqrt{4 + u} + 2)}{u(\sqrt{4 + u} + 2)} = \frac{(4 + u) - 4}{u(\sqrt{4 + u} + 2)} = \frac{u}{u(\sqrt{4 + u} + 2)}$$
3. Cancel $u$:
$$\frac{1}{\sqrt{4 + u} + 2}$$
4. Take the limit as $u \to 0$:
$$\lim_{u \to 0} \frac{1}{\sqrt{4 + u} + 2} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{4} + 2} = \frac{1}{2 + 2} = \frac{1}{4} = 0.25$$
**Final Answer:** $\frac{1}{4}$ (or $0.25$).

---

### Solution to Exercise 3.6
Evaluate $\lim_{x \to 7} \frac{\sqrt{x + 2} - 3}{x - 7}$.
1. At $x = 7$, $\frac{\sqrt{9} - 3}{7 - 7} = \frac{0}{0}$.
2. Multiply by conjugate $(\sqrt{x + 2} + 3)$:
$$\frac{(\sqrt{x + 2} - 3)(\sqrt{x + 2} + 3)}{(x - 7)(\sqrt{x + 2} + 3)} = \frac{(x + 2) - 9}{(x - 7)(\sqrt{x + 2} + 3)} = \frac{x - 7}{(x - 7)(\sqrt{x + 2} + 3)}$$
3. Cancel $(x - 7)$:
$$\frac{1}{\sqrt{x + 2} + 3}$$
4. Take limit as $x \to 7$:
$$\frac{1}{\sqrt{7 + 2} + 3} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{9} + 3} = \frac{1}{3 + 3} = \frac{1}{6}$$
**Final Answer:** $\frac{1}{6}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 3.7
Evaluate $\lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{(2+h)^2} - \frac{1}{4}}{h}$.
1. Direct substitution yields $\frac{\frac{1}{4} - \frac{1}{4}}{0} = \frac{0}{0}$.
2. Find the common denominator for the numerator: $4(2+h)^2$:
$$\frac{1}{(2+h)^2} - \frac{1}{4} = \frac{4 - (2+h)^2}{4(2+h)^2} = \frac{4 - (4 + 4h + h^2)}{4(2+h)^2} = \frac{-4h - h^2}{4(2+h)^2} = \frac{-h(4 + h)}{4(2+h)^2}$$
3. Divide by $h$:
$$\frac{-h(4 + h)}{h \cdot 4(2+h)^2} = \frac{-(4 + h)}{4(2+h)^2}$$
4. Take the limit as $h \to 0$:
$$\lim_{h \to 0} \frac{-(4 + h)}{4(2+h)^2} = \frac{-(4 + 0)}{4(2+0)^2} = \frac{-4}{4(4)} = \frac{-4}{16} = -\frac{1}{4} = -0.25$$
**Final Answer:** $-\frac{1}{4}$ (or $-0.25$).

---

### Solution to Exercise 3.8
Evaluate one-sided limits and continuity for $g(x) = \begin{cases} x^2 + 1, & x < 2 \\ 3x - 1, & x \ge 2 \end{cases}$ at $x = 2$.
1. Left-hand limit ($x \to 2^-$):
$$\lim_{x \to 2^-} g(x) = \lim_{x \to 2^-} (x^2 + 1) = 2^2 + 1 = 4 + 1 = 5$$
2. Right-hand limit ($x \to 2^+$):
$$\lim_{x \to 2^+} g(x) = \lim_{x \to 2^+} (3x - 1) = 3(2) - 1 = 6 - 1 = 5$$
3. Since $\lim_{x \to 2^-} g(x) = 5 = \lim_{x \to 2^+} g(x)$, the two-sided limit exists and equals $5$.
**Final Answer:** Both one-sided limits equal $5$, and the two-sided limit $\lim_{x \to 2} g(x) = 5$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 3.9
Evaluate $\lim_{x \to 0} x^2 \sin^2\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)$ via the Squeeze Theorem.
1. The sine function is bounded: $-1 \le \sin\theta \le 1$. Squaring gives:
$$0 \le \sin^2\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \le 1 \quad \text{for all } x \neq 0$$
2. Multiply by $x^2$ (which is strictly positive for $x \neq 0$):
$$0 \le x^2 \sin^2\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \le x^2$$
3. Evaluate bounding limits as $x \to 0$:
$$\lim_{x \to 0} 0 = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to 0} x^2 = 0$$
4. By the Squeeze Theorem, the trapped term must converge to $0$.
**Final Answer:** $0$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 3.10
Find the equation of the tangent line to $y = \frac{1}{x}$ at $(2, 0.5)$.
1. Contact point: $(x_0, y_0) = (2, \frac{1}{2})$.
2. Tangent slope $m = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(2+h) - f(2)}{h}$:
$$f(2+h) - f(2) = \frac{1}{2+h} - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{2 - (2+h)}{2(2+h)} = \frac{-h}{2(2+h)}$$
$$\frac{f(2+h) - f(2)}{h} = \frac{-h}{h \cdot 2(2+h)} = \frac{-1}{2(2+h)}$$
$$m = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{-1}{2(2+h)} = \frac{-1}{2(2)} = -\frac{1}{4} = -0.25$$
3. Point-slope equation:
$$y - \frac{1}{2} = -\frac{1}{4}(x - 2)$$
$$y - \frac{1}{2} = -\frac{1}{4}x + \frac{1}{2}$$
$$y = -\frac{1}{4}x + 1 \quad \text{or} \quad x + 4y - 4 = 0$$
**Final Answer:** $y = -\frac{1}{4}x + 1$ (slope $m = -0.25$, $y$-intercept $b = 1$).


---\n
# Chapter 4: The Formal Definition of the Derivative â€” First Principles, Tangents, and Notation

> *"The derivative is not an isolated number or an arbitrary algebraic trick. It is a new engine of knowledge: an operator that accepts a function describing the state of the world and outputs a new function describing its rate of change at every conceivable moment."*

---

## 4.1 The Core Definition: From Difference Quotient to Derivative

In Chapters 1 through 3, we traced how average rates of change over finite intervals collapse into instantaneous rates of change through the machinery of limits. Now, we elevate this discovery into the foundational pillar of differential calculus: the **formal definition of the derivative**.

Let $f$ be a real-valued function defined on an open interval containing the point $x$. The **derivative of $f$ at $x$**, denoted by $f'(x)$, is defined by the limit:

$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x + h) - f(x)}{h}$$

provided this limit exists.

If the limit exists, we say that $f$ is **differentiable** at $x$. The process of computing this limit is called **differentiation**.

### The Alternative Point-Formulation
An equivalent definition focuses on a fixed anchor coordinate $a$ and a variable coordinate $x$:

$$f'(a) = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}$$

Notice the exact equivalence: if we define the displacement step $h = x - a$, then $x = a + h$. As $x \to a$, the difference $h \to 0$, converting $\frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}$ directly into $\frac{f(a + h) - f(a)}{h}$. Both formulations are mathematically identical; the $h$-notation is universally favored when deriving general formulas for all $x$, while the $(x - a)$ formulation is exceptionally lucid when proving theoretical theorems about behavior at a specific target point.

---

## 4.2 First-Principles Derivations of Canonical Functions

Before developing shortcut rules in subsequent chapters, a student of mathematics must construct the derivatives of fundamental functions directly from first principles.

### 1. The Constant Function: $f(x) = c$
A constant function never changes. If $f(x) = c$ for all $x$:

$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{c - c}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{0}{h}$$

Because $h \neq 0$ inside the limit, $\frac{0}{h}$ is strictly $0$ before any limit is even evaluated:

$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} 0 = 0$$

*Geometric reality:* A horizontal line has an inclination of zero at every point.

### 2. The Linear Function: $f(x) = mx + b$
For any straight line:

$$f(x+h) = m(x+h) + b = mx + mh + b$$
$$f(x+h) - f(x) = (mx + mh + b) - (mx + b) = mh$$
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{mh}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} m = m$$

*Geometric reality:* The rate of change of a linear graph is its constant slope $m$.

### 3. The Parabolic Function: $f(x) = x^2$
For a squaring operation:

$$f(x+h) = (x+h)^2 = x^2 + 2xh + h^2$$
$$f(x+h) - f(x) = (x^2 + 2xh + h^2) - x^2 = 2xh + h^2 = h(2x + h)$$
$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{h(2x + h)}{h} = 2x + h \quad (\text{for } h \neq 0)$$
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} (2x + h) = 2x + 0 = 2x$$

*Geometric reality:* At $x = 1$, the slope is $2(1) = 2$. At $x = 0$ (the vertex), the slope is $0$. At $x = -3$, the slope is $-6$. The slope changes smoothly and linearly with $x$.

### 4. The Cubic Function: $f(x) = x^3$
Using the binomial expansion $(x+h)^3 = x^3 + 3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3$:

$$f(x+h) - f(x) = (x^3 + 3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3) - x^3 = 3x^2h + 3xh^2 + h^3 = h(3x^2 + 3xh + h^2)$$
$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = 3x^2 + 3xh + h^2$$
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} (3x^2 + 3xh + h^2) = 3x^2 + 0 + 0 = 3x^2$$

### 5. The Square Root Function: $f(x) = \sqrt{x}$ (for $x > 0$)
Using algebraic conjugate multiplication:

$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{\sqrt{x+h} - \sqrt{x}}{h} \cdot \frac{\sqrt{x+h} + \sqrt{x}}{\sqrt{x+h} + \sqrt{x}}$$
$$= \frac{(x+h) - x}{h(\sqrt{x+h} + \sqrt{x})} = \frac{h}{h(\sqrt{x+h} + \sqrt{x})} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+h} + \sqrt{x}}$$
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+h} + \sqrt{x}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x} + \sqrt{x}} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}$$

### 6. The Reciprocal Function: $f(x) = \frac{1}{x}$ (for $x \neq 0$)
Using common denominators:

$$f(x+h) - f(x) = \frac{1}{x+h} - \frac{1}{x} = \frac{x - (x+h)}{x(x+h)} = \frac{-h}{x(x+h)}$$
$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{-h}{h \cdot x(x+h)} = \frac{-1}{x(x+h)}$$
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{-1}{x(x+h)} = \frac{-1}{x^2}$$

---

## 4.3 The Great Mathematical Notations

Calculus was independently discovered by Sir Isaac Newton and Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz in the late 17th century, and subsequently refined by Joseph-Louis Lagrange and Leonhard Euler. Each master introduced a notation emphasizing a unique perspective:

| Notation | Creator | Syntax | Primary Strength & Domain |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Leibniz** | Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz | $\frac{df}{dx}, \frac{dy}{dx}$ | Expresses derivative as an infinitesimal ratio. Dimensional analysis is trivial (units of $y$ over units of $x$). Makes chain rule algebraic: $\frac{dy}{dt} = \frac{dy}{dx}\frac{dx}{dt}$. |
| **Lagrange** | Joseph-Louis Lagrange | $f'(x), f''(x), f^{(n)}(x)$ | Pure functional representation. Clean, concise, emphasizes that the derivative is itself a function of $x$. |
| **Newton** | Sir Isaac Newton | $\dot{x}, \ddot{x}$ | Time derivatives in classical mechanics, orbital mechanics, physics simulations ($\dot{x} = v$, $\ddot{x} = a$). |
| **Euler** | Leonhard Euler | $D f, D_x f$ | Operational calculus and linear differential operators in advanced analysis and differential equations. |

In this treatise and in physics simulation code, we move fluently between Leibniz notation (when tracking physical units and coordinate transforms) and Lagrange notation (when evaluating algebraic functions at specific numerical coordinates).

---

## 4.4 Differentiability Implies Continuity

A critical theorem establishes the relationship between smoothness and unbroken connectivity:

> **Theorem:** If a function $f$ is differentiable at a point $x = c$, then $f$ is continuous at $x = c$.

### Direct Proof
Recall the definition of continuity: $f$ is continuous at $c$ if and only if $\lim_{x \to c} f(x) = f(c)$, which is equivalent to stating:

$$\lim_{x \to c} [f(x) - f(c)] = 0$$

Let us rewrite the difference $f(x) - f(c)$ by multiplying and dividing by the non-zero quantity $(x - c)$ for all $x \neq c$:

$$f(x) - f(c) = \frac{f(x) - f(c)}{x - c} \cdot (x - c)$$

Now take the limit as $x \to c$ on both sides and apply the limit product rule:

$$\lim_{x \to c} [f(x) - f(c)] = \lim_{x \to c} \left[ \frac{f(x) - f(c)}{x - c} \cdot (x - c) \right] = \left( \lim_{x \to c} \frac{f(x) - f(c)}{x - c} \right) \cdot \left( \lim_{x \to c} (x - c) \right)$$

Because $f$ is differentiable at $c$, the first limit exists and equals the finite real number $f'(c)$. The second limit evaluates to $(c - c) = 0$:

$$\lim_{x \to c} [f(x) - f(c)] = f'(c) \cdot 0 = 0$$

Therefore:

$$\lim_{x \to c} f(x) = f(c)$$

This confirms that $f$ is continuous at $c$. $\blacksquare$

### The Crucial Counterexample: Continuity Does NOT Imply Differentiability
While differentiability guarantees continuity, **the reverse is false**. A function can be continuous everywhere without being differentiable.

Consider the absolute value function $f(x) = |x|$ at the origin $c = 0$:
1. $f(0) = |0| = 0$.
2. $\lim_{x \to 0} |x| = 0 = f(0)$. Thus, $f(x)$ is continuous at $x = 0$.
3. Now test differentiability using the difference quotient limit:

$$\frac{f(0 + h) - f(0)}{h} = \frac{|h| - 0}{h} = \frac{|h|}{h}$$

- Approaching from the right ($h \to 0^+$): $h > 0 \implies |h| = h \implies \frac{h}{h} = +1$.
- Approaching from the left ($h \to 0^-$): $h < 0 \implies |h| = -h \implies \frac{-h}{h} = -1$.

Because the left-hand limit ($-1$) does not match the right-hand limit ($+1$), the two-sided limit **does not exist**. The curve possesses a sharp corner or "kink" at $x = 0$. A tangent line cannot settle on a single inclination.

```
       y ^            /
         |           /
         |          /  f(x) = |x|
         |  \      /
         |   \    /
         |    \  /
         +-----*-----> x
             (0,0)  Corner: DNE!
```

### Three Modes of Differentiability Failure
1. **Sharp Corners and Cusps:** Left-hand and right-hand slopes approach different finite numbers (e.g., $f(x) = |x|$).
2. **Vertical Tangents:** The slope grows without bound toward $+\infty$ or $-\infty$ (e.g., $f(x) = x^{1/3}$ at $x = 0$).
3. **Discontinuities:** Any break, jump, or vertical asymptote automatically destroys differentiability.

---

## 4.5 Tangent and Normal Lines to a Curve

At any point $P(x_0, y_0)$ on a differentiable curve $y = f(x)$, where $y_0 = f(x_0)$:

### 1. The Tangent Line
The tangent line passes through $(x_0, y_0)$ with slope $m_{\text{tan}} = f'(x_0)$:

$$y - y_0 = f'(x_0)(x - x_0)$$

### 2. The Normal Line
The **normal line** is perpendicular to the tangent line at the point of contact. In Cartesian geometry, two non-vertical lines are perpendicular if and only if their slopes are negative reciprocals:

$$m_{\text{norm}} \cdot m_{\text{tan}} = -1 \implies m_{\text{norm}} = -\frac{1}{f'(x_0)} \quad (\text{provided } f'(x_0) \neq 0)$$

The equation of the normal line is:

$$y - y_0 = -\frac{1}{f'(x_0)}(x - x_0)$$

In physics simulations (such as collision response against curved surfaces, optical reflection, or ray marching), computing the surface normal vector and normal line equation is fundamental to resolving elastic rebound forces.

---

## 4.6 Ten Progressive Worked Numerical Examples

### Example 4.1: First-Principles Polynomial Derivative
**Problem:** Given $f(x) = 5x^2 - 3x + 8$, compute the derivative function $f'(x)$ using the formal limit definition.

**Step 1:** Write the limit definition:
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}$$

**Step 2:** Expand $f(x+h)$:
$$f(x+h) = 5(x+h)^2 - 3(x+h) + 8 = 5(x^2 + 2xh + h^2) - 3x - 3h + 8$$
$$f(x+h) = 5x^2 + 10xh + 5h^2 - 3x - 3h + 8$$

**Step 3:** Subtract $f(x) = 5x^2 - 3x + 8$:
$$f(x+h) - f(x) = (5x^2 + 10xh + 5h^2 - 3x - 3h + 8) - (5x^2 - 3x + 8)$$
$$= 10xh + 5h^2 - 3h$$

**Step 4:** Factor out the vanishing factor $h$:
$$f(x+h) - f(x) = h(10x + 5h - 3)$$

**Step 5:** Divide by $h$:
$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = 10x + 5h - 3 \quad (\text{for } h \neq 0)$$

**Step 6:** Evaluate the limit as $h \to 0$:
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} (10x + 5h - 3) = 10x + 5(0) - 3 = 10x - 3$$

**Conclusion:** The derivative is $f'(x) = 10x - 3$.

---

### Example 4.2: First-Principles Radical Derivative
**Problem:** Derive the derivative of $f(x) = \sqrt{4x + 1}$ for $x > -1/4$ directly from first principles.

**Step 1:** Construct the difference quotient:
$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{\sqrt{4(x+h) + 1} - \sqrt{4x + 1}}{h} = \frac{\sqrt{4x + 4h + 1} - \sqrt{4x + 1}}{h}$$

**Step 2:** Multiply by the conjugate expression:
$$\frac{\sqrt{4x + 4h + 1} - \sqrt{4x + 1}}{h} \cdot \frac{\sqrt{4x + 4h + 1} + \sqrt{4x + 1}}{\sqrt{4x + 4h + 1} + \sqrt{4x + 1}}$$

**Step 3:** Apply the difference of squares $(a - b)(a + b) = a^2 - b^2$:
$$= \frac{(4x + 4h + 1) - (4x + 1)}{h(\sqrt{4x + 4h + 1} + \sqrt{4x + 1})} = \frac{4h}{h(\sqrt{4x + 4h + 1} + \sqrt{4x + 1})}$$

**Step 4:** Cancel $h \neq 0$:
$$= \frac{4}{\sqrt{4x + 4h + 1} + \sqrt{4x + 1}}$$

**Step 5:** Evaluate the limit as $h \to 0$:
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{4}{\sqrt{4x + 4h + 1} + \sqrt{4x + 1}} = \frac{4}{\sqrt{4x + 1} + \sqrt{4x + 1}} = \frac{4}{2\sqrt{4x + 1}} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{4x + 1}}$$

**Conclusion:** $f'(x) = \frac{2}{\sqrt{4x + 1}}$.

---

### Example 4.3: First-Principles Rational Function Derivative
**Problem:** Derive the derivative of $f(x) = \frac{3}{2x - 1}$ for $x \neq 1/2$.

**Step 1:** Form the difference $f(x+h) - f(x)$:
$$f(x+h) - f(x) = \frac{3}{2(x+h) - 1} - \frac{3}{2x - 1} = \frac{3}{2x + 2h - 1} - \frac{3}{2x - 1}$$

**Step 2:** Find a common denominator:
$$= \frac{3(2x - 1) - 3(2x + 2h - 1)}{(2x + 2h - 1)(2x - 1)} = \frac{6x - 3 - (6x + 6h - 3)}{(2x + 2h - 1)(2x - 1)} = \frac{-6h}{(2x + 2h - 1)(2x - 1)}$$

**Step 3:** Divide by $h$:
$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{-6h}{h(2x + 2h - 1)(2x - 1)} = \frac{-6}{(2x + 2h - 1)(2x - 1)}$$

**Step 4:** Evaluate the limit as $h \to 0$:
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{-6}{(2x + 2h - 1)(2x - 1)} = \frac{-6}{(2x - 1)(2x - 1)} = \frac{-6}{(2x - 1)^2}$$

**Conclusion:** $f'(x) = -\frac{6}{(2x - 1)^2}$.

---

### Example 4.4: Derivative at a Target Point via Alternative Limit
**Problem:** For $f(x) = x^3 - 4x$, calculate $f'(-2)$ using the point formulation $f'(a) = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}$.

**Step 1:** Identify $a = -2$ and calculate $f(a)$:
$$f(-2) = (-2)^3 - 4(-2) = -8 + 8 = 0$$

**Step 2:** Form the difference quotient:
$$\frac{f(x) - f(-2)}{x - (-2)} = \frac{(x^3 - 4x) - 0}{x + 2} = \frac{x(x^2 - 4)}{x + 2}$$

**Step 3:** Factor $x^2 - 4$:
$$\frac{x(x - 2)(x + 2)}{x + 2}$$

**Step 4:** Cancel $(x + 2)$ for $x \neq -2$:
$$= x(x - 2)$$

**Step 5:** Take the limit as $x \to -2$:
$$f'(-2) = \lim_{x \to -2} [x(x - 2)] = (-2)(-2 - 2) = (-2)(-4) = 8$$

**Conclusion:** The instantaneous rate of change at $x = -2$ is $8$.

---

### Example 4.5: Proof of Non-Differentiability at a Sharp Corner
**Problem:** Mathematically prove that $f(x) = |3x - 12|$ is continuous but not differentiable at $x = 4$.

**Step 1:** Test continuity at $x = 4$:
$$f(4) = |3(4) - 12| = |0| = 0$$
$$\lim_{x \to 4} |3x - 12| = |3(4) - 12| = 0 = f(4)$$
The function is continuous at $x = 4$.

**Step 2:** Form the difference quotient at $x = 4$:
$$\frac{f(4 + h) - f(4)}{h} = \frac{|3(4+h) - 12| - 0}{h} = \frac{|12 + 3h - 12|}{h} = \frac{|3h|}{h} = \frac{3|h|}{h}$$

**Step 3:** Evaluate the left-hand limit ($h \to 0^-$):
For $h < 0$, $|h| = -h$:
$$\lim_{h \to 0^-} \frac{3(-h)}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0^-} (-3) = -3$$

**Step 4:** Evaluate the right-hand limit ($h \to 0^+$):
For $h > 0$, $|h| = +h$:
$$\lim_{h \to 0^+} \frac{3(h)}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0^+} (+3) = +3$$

**Step 5:** Conclude:
Because $\lim_{h \to 0^-} \neq \lim_{h \to 0^+}$ ($-3 \neq +3$), the two-sided limit does not exist. $f$ is not differentiable at $x = 4$.

---

### Example 4.6: Investigation of a Vertical Tangent
**Problem:** Investigate the differentiability of $f(x) = x^{1/3}$ at $x = 0$.

**Step 1:** Construct the difference quotient at $x = 0$:
$$\frac{f(0 + h) - f(0)}{h} = \frac{h^{1/3} - 0}{h} = \frac{h^{1/3}}{h^1} = \frac{1}{h^{2/3}}$$

**Step 2:** Take the limit as $h \to 0$:
Notice that $h^{2/3} = (\sqrt[3]{h})^2 \ge 0$ for all real $h \neq 0$.
As $h \to 0$, $h^{2/3}$ is positive and approaches zero.
$$\lim_{h \to 0} \frac{1}{h^{2/3}} = +\infty$$

**Conclusion:** The limit does not yield a finite real number; the slope approaches positive infinity from both sides. The curve has a **vertical tangent** at the origin, meaning $f$ is not differentiable at $x = 0$.

---

### Example 4.7: Tangent and Normal Lines to a Square Root Curve
**Problem:** For $f(x) = \sqrt{x}$, determine the equations of both the tangent line and the normal line at $x_0 = 9$.

**Step 1:** Determine the contact point $(x_0, y_0)$:
$$y_0 = f(9) = \sqrt{9} = 3 \implies P(9, 3)$$

**Step 2:** Compute the derivative at $x_0 = 9$ using $f'(x) = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}$:
$$m_{\text{tan}} = f'(9) = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{9}} = \frac{1}{2(3)} = \frac{1}{6}$$

**Step 3:** Formulate the tangent line equation:
$$y - 3 = \frac{1}{6}(x - 9) \implies y - 3 = \frac{1}{6}x - \frac{9}{6} = \frac{1}{6}x - \frac{3}{2}$$
$$y = \frac{1}{6}x + \frac{3}{2} \quad \text{or} \quad x - 6y + 9 = 0$$

**Step 4:** Formulate the normal line equation:
The normal slope is $m_{\text{norm}} = -\frac{1}{m_{\text{tan}}} = -\frac{1}{1/6} = -6$:
$$y - 3 = -6(x - 9) \implies y - 3 = -6x + 54$$
$$y = -6x + 57 \quad \text{or} \quad 6x + y - 57 = 0$$

---

### Example 4.8: Rocket Telemetry Instantaneous Velocity
**Problem:** A sounding rocket's altitude in meters during the first stage burn is given by $s(t) = 4t^3 + 2t^2$. Using first principles, determine its velocity at $t = 2$ seconds.

**Step 1:** Recall that instantaneous velocity is the derivative of position: $v(t) = s'(t) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{s(t+h) - s(t)}{h}$.

**Step 2:** Evaluate $s(2)$:
$$s(2) = 4(2)^3 + 2(2)^2 = 4(8) + 2(4) = 32 + 8 = 40\text{ m}$$

**Step 3:** Expand $s(2+h)$:
$$s(2+h) = 4(2+h)^3 + 2(2+h)^2$$
$$= 4(8 + 12h + 6h^2 + h^3) + 2(4 + 4h + h^2)$$
$$= (32 + 48h + 24h^2 + 4h^3) + (8 + 8h + 2h^2)$$
$$= 40 + 56h + 26h^2 + 4h^3$$

**Step 4:** Subtract $s(2) = 40$:
$$s(2+h) - s(2) = 56h + 26h^2 + 4h^3 = h(56 + 26h + 4h^2)$$

**Step 5:** Divide by $h$ and take the limit:
$$v(2) = \lim_{h \to 0} (56 + 26h + 4h^2) = 56 + 0 + 0 = 56\text{ m/s}$$

**Conclusion:** At $t = 2$ seconds, the rocket's instantaneous vertical velocity is $56\text{ m/s}$.

---

### Example 4.9: Finding Horizontal Tangents
**Problem:** Find all points on the parabola $f(x) = x^2 - 6x + 8$ where the tangent line is horizontal.

**Step 1:** A horizontal line has slope $m = 0$. Therefore, we must find all $x$ where $f'(x) = 0$.

**Step 2:** Compute $f'(x)$ from first principles:
$$f(x+h) = (x+h)^2 - 6(x+h) + 8 = x^2 + 2xh + h^2 - 6x - 6h + 8$$
$$f(x+h) - f(x) = 2xh + h^2 - 6h = h(2x + h - 6)$$
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} (2x + h - 6) = 2x - 6$$

**Step 3:** Set $f'(x) = 0$:
$$2x - 6 = 0 \implies 2x = 6 \implies x = 3$$

**Step 4:** Find the corresponding $y$-coordinate:
$$y = f(3) = 3^2 - 6(3) + 8 = 9 - 18 + 8 = -1$$

**Conclusion:** The tangent line is horizontal at exactly one point: $(3, -1)$ (the vertex of the parabola).

---

### Example 4.10: Demonstrating a Cusp (Continuous Without Derivative)
**Problem:** Examine the curve $f(x) = x^{2/3}$ at $x = 0$. Determine if it is continuous and whether $f'(0)$ exists.

**Step 1:** Continuity at $x = 0$:
$f(0) = 0^{2/3} = 0$.
$\lim_{x \to 0} x^{2/3} = 0 = f(0)$.
The function is continuous at the origin.

**Step 2:** Evaluate the difference quotient for the derivative at $x = 0$:
$$\frac{f(0+h) - f(0)}{h} = \frac{h^{2/3} - 0}{h} = \frac{h^{2/3}}{h^1} = \frac{1}{h^{1/3}}$$

**Step 3:** Evaluate one-sided limits of $\frac{1}{h^{1/3}}$:
- As $h \to 0^+$: $h$ is positive, so $h^{1/3} > 0 \implies \lim_{h \to 0^+} \frac{1}{h^{1/3}} = +\infty$.
- As $h \to 0^-$: $h$ is negative, so $h^{1/3} < 0 \implies \lim_{h \to 0^-} \frac{1}{h^{1/3}} = -\infty$.

**Step 4:** Physical and geometric interpretation:
From the right, the tangent line becomes infinitely steep in the upward direction ($+\infty$). From the left, it becomes infinitely steep in the downward direction ($-\infty$). The curve forms a sharp, needle-like point called a **cusp**.

**Conclusion:** $f(x) = x^{2/3}$ is continuous at $x = 0$, but $f'(0)$ does not exist.

---

## 4.7 Chapter Mastery Problem Set

Complete step-by-step written solutions are provided immediately following the exercises.

### Tier 1: Polynomial First-Principles Derivations
- **Exercise 4.1:** Using the formal definition $f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}$, derive the derivative of $f(x) = 7x + 12$.
- **Exercise 4.2:** Derive the derivative of $f(x) = 3x^2 - 4x$ from first principles.

### Tier 2: Radicals and Fractions via First Principles
- **Exercise 4.3:** Derive the derivative of $f(x) = \sqrt{2x + 5}$ from first principles.
- **Exercise 4.4:** Derive the derivative of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x+2}$ from first principles for $x \neq -2$.

### Tier 3: Tangent and Normal Line Construction
- **Exercise 4.5:** Find the equation of the tangent line to the curve $y = 2x^2 - 5x + 1$ at $x = 2$.
- **Exercise 4.6:** Find the equation of the normal line to the curve $y = x^3$ at $x = 1$.

### Tier 4: Kinematics and Physical Rates
- **Exercise 4.7:** A particle moves along a straight rail such that its position in meters is $s(t) = 5t^2 - 20t + 15$. Find the exact time $t$ when the particle momentarily comes to rest (velocity $v = 0$).
- **Exercise 4.8:** The surface area of an expanding spherical weather balloon is $A(r) = 4\pi r^2$. Find the instantaneous rate of change of surface area with respect to radius $r$ when $r = 5\text{ m}$.

### Tier 5: Theoretical Analysis & Rigorous Proofs
- **Exercise 4.9:** Let $f(x) = \begin{cases} x^2 \sin(1/x), & x \neq 0 \\ 0, & x = 0 \end{cases}$. Prove using the limit definition of the derivative that $f'(0)$ exists and find its value.
- **Exercise 4.10:** Prove that if $f(x) = |x - 2| + |x + 2|$, the derivative $f'(x)$ is equal to $0$ for all $x \in (-2, 2)$.

---

## 4.8 Complete Step-by-Step Solutions to Chapter Exercises

### Solution to Exercise 4.1
Derive the derivative of $f(x) = 7x + 12$ from first principles.
1. Form $f(x+h)$:
$$f(x+h) = 7(x+h) + 12 = 7x + 7h + 12$$
2. Subtract $f(x)$:
$$f(x+h) - f(x) = (7x + 7h + 12) - (7x + 12) = 7h$$
3. Divide by $h$:
$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{7h}{h} = 7 \quad (\text{for } h \neq 0)$$
4. Take the limit:
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} 7 = 7$$
**Final Answer:** $f'(x) = 7$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 4.2
Derive the derivative of $f(x) = 3x^2 - 4x$ from first principles.
1. Form $f(x+h)$:
$$f(x+h) = 3(x+h)^2 - 4(x+h) = 3(x^2 + 2xh + h^2) - 4x - 4h = 3x^2 + 6xh + 3h^2 - 4x - 4h$$
2. Subtract $f(x) = 3x^2 - 4x$:
$$f(x+h) - f(x) = 6xh + 3h^2 - 4h = h(6x + 3h - 4)$$
3. Divide by $h$:
$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = 6x + 3h - 4$$
4. Take the limit as $h \to 0$:
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} (6x + 3h - 4) = 6x - 4$$
**Final Answer:** $f'(x) = 6x - 4$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 4.3
Derive the derivative of $f(x) = \sqrt{2x + 5}$ from first principles.
1. Form the difference quotient:
$$\frac{\sqrt{2(x+h) + 5} - \sqrt{2x + 5}}{h} = \frac{\sqrt{2x + 2h + 5} - \sqrt{2x + 5}}{h}$$
2. Multiply by the conjugate:
$$\frac{(\sqrt{2x + 2h + 5} - \sqrt{2x + 5})(\sqrt{2x + 2h + 5} + \sqrt{2x + 5})}{h(\sqrt{2x + 2h + 5} + \sqrt{2x + 5})}$$
3. Expand numerator:
$$= \frac{(2x + 2h + 5) - (2x + 5)}{h(\sqrt{2x + 2h + 5} + \sqrt{2x + 5})} = \frac{2h}{h(\sqrt{2x + 2h + 5} + \sqrt{2x + 5})}$$
4. Cancel $h$:
$$= \frac{2}{\sqrt{2x + 2h + 5} + \sqrt{2x + 5}}$$
5. Evaluate limit as $h \to 0$:
$$f'(x) = \frac{2}{\sqrt{2x + 5} + \sqrt{2x + 5}} = \frac{2}{2\sqrt{2x + 5}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2x + 5}}$$
**Final Answer:** $f'(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2x + 5}}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 4.4
Derive the derivative of $f(x) = \frac{1}{x+2}$ from first principles.
1. Difference in numerator:
$$f(x+h) - f(x) = \frac{1}{x+h+2} - \frac{1}{x+2} = \frac{(x+2) - (x+h+2)}{(x+h+2)(x+2)} = \frac{-h}{(x+h+2)(x+2)}$$
2. Divide by $h$:
$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{-h}{h(x+h+2)(x+2)} = \frac{-1}{(x+h+2)(x+2)}$$
3. Take limit as $h \to 0$:
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{-1}{(x+h+2)(x+2)} = \frac{-1}{(x+2)^2}$$
**Final Answer:** $f'(x) = -\frac{1}{(x+2)^2}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 4.5
Find the tangent line to $y = 2x^2 - 5x + 1$ at $x = 2$.
1. Find $y_0 = f(2)$:
$$y_0 = 2(2)^2 - 5(2) + 1 = 2(4) - 10 + 1 = 8 - 10 + 1 = -1 \implies P(2, -1)$$
2. Compute derivative $f'(x)$:
From $f(x) = 2x^2 - 5x + 1$, $f'(x) = 4x - 5$.
3. Evaluate slope at $x = 2$:
$$m = f'(2) = 4(2) - 5 = 8 - 5 = 3$$
4. Point-slope equation:
$$y - (-1) = 3(x - 2) \implies y + 1 = 3x - 6 \implies y = 3x - 7$$
**Final Answer:** $y = 3x - 7$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 4.6
Find the normal line to $y = x^3$ at $x = 1$.
1. Contact point: $y_0 = 1^3 = 1 \implies P(1, 1)$.
2. Tangent slope: $f'(x) = 3x^2 \implies m_{\text{tan}} = 3(1)^2 = 3$.
3. Normal slope: $m_{\text{norm}} = -\frac{1}{m_{\text{tan}}} = -\frac{1}{3}$.
4. Line equation:
$$y - 1 = -\frac{1}{3}(x - 1) \implies y - 1 = -\frac{1}{3}x + \frac{1}{3} \implies y = -\frac{1}{3}x + \frac{4}{3}$$
**Final Answer:** $y = -\frac{1}{3}x + \frac{4}{3}$ (or $x + 3y - 4 = 0$).

---

### Solution to Exercise 4.7
Find when the particle $s(t) = 5t^2 - 20t + 15$ comes to rest.
1. Velocity is $v(t) = s'(t)$.
2. Differentiate $s(t)$:
$$s'(t) = 10t - 20$$
3. Set $v(t) = 0$:
$$10t - 20 = 0 \implies 10t = 20 \implies t = 2\text{ s}$$
**Final Answer:** $t = 2$ seconds.

---

### Solution to Exercise 4.8
Find $\frac{dA}{dr}$ for $A(r) = 4\pi r^2$ at $r = 5\text{ m}$.
1. Differentiate with respect to $r$:
$$\frac{dA}{dr} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{4\pi(r+h)^2 - 4\pi r^2}{h} = 4\pi \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{2rh + h^2}{h} = 4\pi(2r) = 8\pi r$$
2. Evaluate at $r = 5$:
$$\left.\frac{dA}{dr}\right|_{r=5} = 8\pi(5) = 40\pi \approx 125.66\text{ m}^2/\text{m}$$
**Final Answer:** $40\pi\text{ m}^2/\text{m}$ (or $\approx 125.66\text{ m}$).

---

### Solution to Exercise 4.9
Evaluate $f'(0)$ for $f(x) = \begin{cases} x^2 \sin(1/x), & x \neq 0 \\ 0, & x = 0 \end{cases}$.
1. Apply the formal limit definition at $x = 0$:
$$f'(0) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(0 + h) - f(0)}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{h^2 \sin(1/h) - 0}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} [h \sin(1/h)]$$
2. Apply the Squeeze Theorem:
Since $-1 \le \sin(1/h) \le 1$, for any $h$:
$$-|h| \le h \sin(1/h) \le |h|$$
3. As $h \to 0$, both $-|h| \to 0$ and $|h| \to 0$.
4. Therefore, $\lim_{h \to 0} [h \sin(1/h)] = 0$.
**Final Answer:** $f'(0)$ exists and equals $0$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 4.10
Prove that for $f(x) = |x - 2| + |x + 2|$, $f'(x) = 0$ for all $x \in (-2, 2)$.
1. For any $x \in (-2, 2)$:
Since $x > -2$, $x + 2 > 0 \implies |x + 2| = x + 2$.
Since $x < 2$, $x - 2 < 0 \implies |x - 2| = -(x - 2) = 2 - x$.
2. Substitute these into $f(x)$:
$$f(x) = (2 - x) + (x + 2) = 4$$
3. For every $x \in (-2, 2)$, $f(x)$ is the constant function $f(x) = 4$.
4. The derivative of a constant function is $0$:
$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{4 - 4}{h} = 0$$
**Final Answer:** For all $x \in (-2, 2)$, $f(x) \equiv 4$, therefore $f'(x) = 0$.


---\n
# Chapter 5: Linearity, the Power Rule, and Polynomial Calculus

> *"In the dawn of calculus, finding the slope of a curve demanded a painstaking siege of algebraic manipulation and vanishing steps for every single equation. The discovery of operational rules transformed this grueling labor into swift, mechanical calculation â€” turning what was once a creative ordeal into an exact, reproducible science."*

---

## 5.1 The Transition from First Principles to Operational Rules

In Chapter 4, we saw that evaluating the formal limit:

$$f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h}$$

from scratch for every function is computationally exhaustive. Even for modest polynomials, expanding powers of $(x+h)$ generates sprawling binomial coefficients.

Fortunately, mathematics is the art of recognizing structural invariants. When we analyze the limit definition applied to common functional forms, universal algebraic patterns emerge. Once these patterns are proven once and for all, they become **operational derivative rules**. These rules allow an engineer or scientist to differentiate any polynomial or rational power expression in seconds with 100% precision.

---

## 5.2 The Foundations of Linearity

Differentiation is a **linear operator**. In advanced mathematics and physics simulation, an operator $L$ is called *linear* if it satisfies two inviolable axioms:
1. **Additivity:** $L[f + g] = L[f] + L[g]$
2. **Homogeneity (Scalar Multiplication):** $L[c \cdot f] = c \cdot L[f]$

Let us prove both axioms directly from the fundamental limit laws.

### Theorem 1: The Constant Rule
For any real constant $c \in \mathbb{R}$:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[c] = 0$$

*Proof:* Let $f(x) = c$. Then $f(x+h) = c$.
$$\frac{d}{dx}[c] = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{c - c}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{0}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} 0 = 0 \quad \blacksquare$$

### Theorem 2: The Constant Multiple Rule
If $f(x)$ is differentiable and $c \in \mathbb{R}$ is any constant scalar:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[c \cdot f(x)] = c \cdot \frac{df}{dx} = c \cdot f'(x)$$

*Proof:* Let $g(x) = c \cdot f(x)$.
$$\frac{d}{dx}[g(x)] = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{c \cdot f(x+h) - c \cdot f(x)}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} c \cdot \left[ \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} \right]$$
By the constant multiple limit law, the scalar $c$ factors outside the limit:
$$= c \cdot \left( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} \right) = c \cdot f'(x) \quad \blacksquare$$

### Theorem 3: The Sum and Difference Rules
If $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ are both differentiable at $x$:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[f(x) \pm g(x)] = f'(x) \pm g'(x)$$

*Proof:* Let $S(x) = f(x) + g(x)$.
$$\frac{dS}{dx} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{[f(x+h) + g(x+h)] - [f(x) + g(x)]}{h}$$
Regroup the terms in the numerator by function:
$$= \lim_{h \to 0} \left[ \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} + \frac{g(x+h) - g(x)}{h} \right]$$
By the limit sum law, the limit of a sum is the sum of the limits:
$$= \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} + \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{g(x+h) - g(x)}{h} = f'(x) + g'(x) \quad \blacksquare$$
An identical argument using a subtraction sign establishes the difference rule.

Together, these rules mean we can differentiate any sum of terms term-by-term, pulling constant multipliers along untouched!

---

## 5.3 The Sovereign Power Rule

The most famous shortcut in all of calculus is the **Power Rule**:

$$\frac{d}{dx}\left[x^n\right] = n x^{n-1}$$

This formula holds true for **any real number exponent $n$**: positive integers, zero, negative integers, rational fractions, and irrational numbers alike!

### Proof for Positive Integers $n \in \mathbb{N}$ via the Binomial Theorem
Recall Newton's Binomial Expansion for $(x+h)^n$:

$$(x+h)^n = \sum_{k=0}^{n} \binom{n}{k} x^{n-k} h^k = x^n + \binom{n}{1} x^{n-1} h + \binom{n}{2} x^{n-2} h^2 + \dots + h^n$$

Notice that the binomial coefficient $\binom{n}{1} = n$. Substituting this into the formal derivative limit:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[x^n] = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{(x+h)^n - x^n}{h}$$
$$= \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\left( x^n + n x^{n-1} h + \binom{n}{2} x^{n-2} h^2 + \dots + h^n \right) - x^n}{h}$$

The leading $x^n$ terms cancel exactly:

$$= \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{n x^{n-1} h + \binom{n}{2} x^{n-2} h^2 + \dots + h^n}{h}$$

Factor out $h$ from every remaining term in the numerator:

$$= \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{h \cdot \left[ n x^{n-1} + \binom{n}{2} x^{n-2} h + \dots + h^{n-1} \right]}{h}$$

For all $h \neq 0$, cancel $h$:

$$= \lim_{h \to 0} \left[ n x^{n-1} + \binom{n}{2} x^{n-2} h + \dots + h^{n-1} \right]$$

Now take the limit as $h \to 0$. Every single term after the first contains at least one factor of $h$, meaning every subsequent term vanishes into zero:

$$= n x^{n-1} + 0 + 0 + \dots + 0 = n x^{n-1} \quad \blacksquare$$

### Extension to Fractional and Negative Powers
Consider $f(x) = x^{1/2} = \sqrt{x}$. Applying the power rule with $n = \frac{1}{2}$:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[x^{1/2}] = \frac{1}{2} x^{\frac{1}{2} - 1} = \frac{1}{2} x^{-1/2} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}$$

This matches our first-principles conjugate derivation in Chapter 4 down to the exact digit!

Consider $f(x) = x^{-1} = \frac{1}{x}$. Applying the power rule with $n = -1$:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[x^{-1}] = (-1) x^{-1 - 1} = -1 x^{-2} = -\frac{1}{x^2}$$

This matches our first-principles common-denominator derivation in Chapter 4!

---

## 5.4 Strategic Algebraic Preparation: Rewriting Before Differentiating

In differential calculus, students often make errors not in calculus, but in pre-calculus algebra. Many expressions that appear complicated can be converted into standard power form $k x^n$ prior to applying any derivative rule:

| Expression | Algebraic Rewrite ($k x^n$) | Power Rule Derivative | Simplified Form |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $f(x) = \frac{1}{x^5}$ | $x^{-5}$ | $-5 x^{-5-1} = -5 x^{-6}$ | $-\frac{5}{x^6}$ |
| $f(x) = \sqrt[3]{x}$ | $x^{1/3}$ | $\frac{1}{3} x^{\frac{1}{3} - 1} = \frac{1}{3} x^{-2/3}$ | $\frac{1}{3\sqrt[3]{x^2}}$ |
| $f(x) = \frac{4}{\sqrt{x}}$ | $4 x^{-1/2}$ | $4 \left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) x^{-\frac{1}{2} - 1} = -2 x^{-3/2}$ | $-\frac{2}{x\sqrt{x}}$ |
| $f(x) = \frac{6x^3 - 4x}{x}$ | $6x^2 - 4$ | $6(2x) - 0 = 12x$ | $12x$ |
| $f(x) = (2x - 3)^2$ | $4x^2 - 12x + 9$ | $4(2x) - 12(1) + 0 = 8x - 12$ | $8x - 12$ |

> [!TIP]
> **Golden Rule of Polynomial Calculus:** Whenever possible, expand products and divide through by monomial denominators **before** applying derivative rules. Transforming an algebraic quotient into a sum of simple powers turns a complicated quotient calculation into basic power-rule operations.

---

## 5.5 Higher-Order Derivatives and Nilpotence

Because the derivative of a function $f(x)$ is itself a function $f'(x)$, we can differentiate it again!

- **First Derivative:** $f'(x) = \frac{dy}{dx}$ (Rate of change of $y$)
- **Second Derivative:** $f''(x) = \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{dy}{dx}\right]$ (Rate of change of the rate of change; acceleration, concavity)
- **Third Derivative:** $f'''(x) = \frac{d^3y}{dx^3} = \frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}\right]$ (Jerk)
- **$n$-th Derivative:** $f^{(n)}(x) = \frac{d^ny}{dx^n}$

### The Nilpotent Property of Polynomials
Notice what happens to the power of $x$ at each differentiation step: it decreases by $1$.
For a degree-$n$ polynomial:

$$P(x) = a_n x^n + a_{n-1} x^{n-1} + \dots + a_1 x + a_0$$

- $P'(x)$ has degree $n - 1$.
- $P''(x)$ has degree $n - 2$.
- The $n$-th derivative $P^{(n)}(x)$ is a constant: $P^{(n)}(x) = n! \cdot a_n$.
- The $(n+1)$-th derivative and all subsequent derivatives are **identically zero**:

$$\frac{d^{n+1}}{dx^{n+1}}[P(x)] \equiv 0$$

In algebra, an operator that drives elements to zero after repeated applications is called **nilpotent**. The differentiation operator is nilpotent on the vector space of polynomials!

---

## 5.6 Ten Progressive Worked Numerical Examples

### Example 5.1: Differentiating an 8th-Degree Polynomial
**Problem:** Differentiate $f(x) = \frac{1}{8}x^8 - \frac{2}{5}x^5 + 4x^3 - 7x + 19$.

**Step 1:** Apply linearity: differentiate term by term.
$$f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{1}{8}x^8\right] - \frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{2}{5}x^5\right] + \frac{d}{dx}[4x^3] - \frac{d}{dx}[7x] + \frac{d}{dx}[19]$$

**Step 2:** Apply the Constant Multiple and Power Rules:
- $\frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{1}{8}x^8\right] = \frac{1}{8} \cdot (8x^7) = x^7$
- $\frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{2}{5}x^5\right] = \frac{2}{5} \cdot (5x^4) = 2x^4$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[4x^3] = 4 \cdot (3x^2) = 12x^2$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[7x] = 7 \cdot (1x^0) = 7(1) = 7$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[19] = 0$

**Step 3:** Assemble the derivative:
$$f'(x) = x^7 - 2x^4 + 12x^2 - 7$$

---

### Example 5.2: Rewriting and Differentiating Fractional and Radical Powers
**Problem:** Differentiate $g(x) = 6\sqrt[3]{x^2} - \frac{5}{\sqrt{x}} + 2x^{-4}$.

**Step 1:** Rewrite each term as a standard power $k x^n$:
- $6\sqrt[3]{x^2} = 6(x^{2/3}) = 6x^{2/3}$
- $\frac{5}{\sqrt{x}} = \frac{5}{x^{1/2}} = 5x^{-1/2}$
- $2x^{-4} = 2x^{-4}$

$$g(x) = 6x^{2/3} - 5x^{-1/2} + 2x^{-4}$$

**Step 2:** Differentiate each term using $\frac{d}{dx}[x^n] = n x^{n-1}$:
- $\frac{d}{dx}[6x^{2/3}] = 6 \cdot \left(\frac{2}{3}\right) x^{\frac{2}{3} - 1} = 4 x^{-1/3} = \frac{4}{\sqrt[3]{x}}$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[-5x^{-1/2}] = -5 \cdot \left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) x^{-\frac{1}{2} - 1} = \frac{5}{2} x^{-3/2} = \frac{5}{2x^{3/2}} = \frac{5}{2x\sqrt{x}}$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[2x^{-4}] = 2 \cdot (-4) x^{-4 - 1} = -8 x^{-5} = -\frac{8}{x^5}$

**Step 3:** Recombine into standard radical/fractional form:
$$g'(x) = \frac{4}{\sqrt[3]{x}} + \frac{5}{2x\sqrt{x}} - \frac{8}{x^5}$$

---

### Example 5.3: Splitting a Multi-Term Quotient
**Problem:** Differentiate $y = \frac{4x^5 - 9x^3 + 2x^2 - 7}{x^2}$ for $x \neq 0$.

**Step 1:** Divide each term in the numerator by the monomial denominator $x^2$:
$$y = \frac{4x^5}{x^2} - \frac{9x^3}{x^2} + \frac{2x^2}{x^2} - \frac{7}{x^2} = 4x^3 - 9x + 2 - 7x^{-2}$$

**Step 2:** Differentiate term by term using power rules:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = 4(3x^2) - 9(1) + 0 - 7(-2x^{-3})$$
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = 12x^2 - 9 + 14x^{-3} = 12x^2 - 9 + \frac{14}{x^3}$$

---

### Example 5.4: Successive Derivatives of a Quintic Polynomial
**Problem:** For $f(x) = 2x^5 - 5x^4 + 3x^2 - 11$, compute all derivatives from the 1st through the 6th.

- **1st Derivative:** $f'(x) = 2(5x^4) - 5(4x^3) + 3(2x) - 0 = 10x^4 - 20x^3 + 6x$
- **2nd Derivative:** $f''(x) = 10(4x^3) - 20(3x^2) + 6(1) = 40x^3 - 60x^2 + 6$
- **3rd Derivative:** $f'''(x) = 40(3x^2) - 60(2x) + 0 = 120x^2 - 120x$
- **4th Derivative:** $f^{(4)}(x) = 120(2x) - 120(1) = 240x - 120$
- **5th Derivative:** $f^{(5)}(x) = 240(1) - 0 = 240 = 5! \cdot 2 = 120 \cdot 2$
- **6th Derivative:** $f^{(6)}(x) = \frac{d}{dx}[240] = 0$

All derivatives of order $n \ge 6$ are identically zero.

---

### Example 5.5: Finding Points with Specified Tangent Slope
**Problem:** Find all points on the cubic curve $y = x^3 - 9x^2 + 15x - 4$ where the tangent line has a slope of $m = -12$.

**Step 1:** Compute the slope function $\frac{dy}{dx}$:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = 3x^2 - 18x + 15$$

**Step 2:** Set $\frac{dy}{dx} = -12$:
$$3x^2 - 18x + 15 = -12$$
$$3x^2 - 18x + 27 = 0$$

**Step 3:** Divide through by $3$:
$$x^2 - 6x + 9 = 0$$

**Step 4:** Factor the perfect square:
$$(x - 3)^2 = 0 \implies x = 3$$

**Step 5:** Determine the corresponding $y$-coordinate on the curve:
$$y(3) = 3^3 - 9(3)^2 + 15(3) - 4 = 27 - 9(9) + 45 - 4 = 27 - 81 + 45 - 4 = -13$$

**Conclusion:** The tangent line has slope $-12$ at exactly one point: $(3, -13)$.

---

### Example 5.6: Tangent Line Parallel to a Target Line
**Problem:** Find the equation of the tangent line to $f(x) = 2x^3 - 4x + 1$ that is parallel to the line $y = 20x - 7$.

**Step 1:** Identify the required slope:
The target line $y = 20x - 7$ has slope $m = 20$. Parallel lines have equal slopes, so we require $f'(x) = 20$.

**Step 2:** Differentiate $f(x)$:
$$f'(x) = 6x^2 - 4$$

**Step 3:** Solve for $x$:
$$6x^2 - 4 = 20 \implies 6x^2 = 24 \implies x^2 = 4 \implies x = \pm 2$$

There are two distinct contact points!

**Step 4:** Case A: $x_1 = 2$:
$$y_1 = f(2) = 2(2)^3 - 4(2) + 1 = 2(8) - 8 + 1 = 16 - 8 + 1 = 9 \implies P_1(2, 9)$$
Tangent line at $P_1$:
$$y - 9 = 20(x - 2) \implies y = 20x - 40 + 9 \implies y = 20x - 31$$

**Step 5:** Case B: $x_2 = -2$:
$$y_2 = f(-2) = 2(-2)^3 - 4(-2) + 1 = 2(-8) + 8 + 1 = -16 + 8 + 1 = -7 \implies P_2(-2, -7)$$
Tangent line at $P_2$:
$$y - (-7) = 20(x - (-2)) \implies y + 7 = 20x + 40 \implies y = 20x + 33$$

**Conclusion:** Two parallel tangent lines exist: $y = 20x - 31$ and $y = 20x + 33$.

---

### Example 5.7: Structural Engineering Beam Deflection
**Problem:** In civil engineering, the deflection curve of a cantilever beam of length $L = 6\text{ m}$ carrying a uniform distributed load is:
$$w(x) = \frac{w_0}{24EI}\left(x^4 - 4Lx^3 + 6L^2x^2\right)$$
where $E, I, w_0$ are structural constants. Find the expression for the shear force, which is proportional to the third derivative: $V(x) = -EI \frac{d^3w}{dx^3}$.

**Step 1:** Let $C = \frac{w_0}{24EI}$.
$$w(x) = C(x^4 - 24x^3 + 216x^2) \quad (\text{using } L = 6)$$

**Step 2:** First derivative (slope of deflection):
$$\frac{dw}{dx} = C(4x^3 - 72x^2 + 432x)$$

**Step 3:** Second derivative (bending moment curvature):
$$\frac{d^2w}{dx^2} = C(12x^2 - 144x + 432)$$

**Step 4:** Third derivative:
$$\frac{d^3w}{dx^3} = C(24x - 144) = \frac{w_0}{24EI}(24x - 144) = \frac{w_0}{EI}(x - 6)$$

**Step 5:** Calculate the shear force $V(x)$:
$$V(x) = -EI \frac{d^3w}{dx^3} = -EI \left[\frac{w_0}{EI}(x - 6)\right] = -w_0(x - 6) = w_0(6 - x)$$

**Conclusion:** At the built-in support ($x = 0$), shear force is maximum: $V(0) = 6w_0$. At the free tip ($x = 6$), shear force drops to zero: $V(6) = 0$.

---

### Example 5.8: Evaluating Fractional Derivatives at a Specified Coordinate
**Problem:** For $f(x) = 32x^{-1/2} + 3x^{5/4}$, calculate the exact numerical value of $f'(16)$.

**Step 1:** Differentiate each term using the power rule:
$$f'(x) = 32\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)x^{-\frac{1}{2} - 1} + 3\left(\frac{5}{4}\right)x^{\frac{5}{4} - 1}$$
$$f'(x) = -16 x^{-3/2} + \frac{15}{4} x^{1/4}$$

**Step 2:** Substitute $x = 16$:
$$16^{1/4} = \sqrt[4]{16} = 2$$
$$16^{-3/2} = \frac{1}{(16^{1/2})^3} = \frac{1}{4^3} = \frac{1}{64}$$

**Step 3:** Calculate numerical terms:
$$-16 x^{-3/2} = -16 \cdot \frac{1}{64} = -\frac{16}{64} = -\frac{1}{4} = -0.25$$
$$\frac{15}{4} x^{1/4} = \frac{15}{4} \cdot 2 = \frac{30}{4} = \frac{15}{2} = 7.5$$

**Step 4:** Add the values:
$$f'(16) = -0.25 + 7.50 = 7.25 = \frac{29}{4}$$

**Conclusion:** $f'(16) = \frac{29}{4} = 7.25$.

---

### Example 5.9: Physics Simulator Kinematics Hierarchy
**Problem:** In a 2D physics engine, a projectile follows horizontal position $x(t) = \frac{1}{6}t^4 - t^3 + 3t^2 + 8$ meters. Compute position, velocity, and acceleration at $t = 3$ seconds.

**Step 1:** Position at $t = 3$:
$$x(3) = \frac{1}{6}(3^4) - (3^3) + 3(3^2) + 8 = \frac{81}{6} - 27 + 27 + 8 = 13.5 + 8 = 21.5\text{ m}$$

**Step 2:** Velocity $v(t) = x'(t)$:
$$v(t) = \frac{d}{dt}\left[\frac{1}{6}t^4 - t^3 + 3t^2 + 8\right] = \frac{4}{6}t^3 - 3t^2 + 6t = \frac{2}{3}t^3 - 3t^2 + 6t$$
Evaluate at $t = 3$:
$$v(3) = \frac{2}{3}(27) - 3(9) + 6(3) = 18 - 27 + 18 = 9\text{ m/s}$$

**Step 3:** Acceleration $a(t) = v'(t) = x''(t)$:
$$a(t) = \frac{d}{dt}\left[\frac{2}{3}t^3 - 3t^2 + 6t\right] = \frac{2}{3}(3t^2) - 3(2t) + 6 = 2t^2 - 6t + 6$$
Evaluate at $t = 3$:
$$a(3) = 2(3^2) - 6(3) + 6 = 2(9) - 18 + 6 = 18 - 18 + 6 = 6\text{ m/s}^2$$

**Conclusion:** At $t = 3\text{ s}$: position is $21.5\text{ m}$, velocity is $9\text{ m/s}$, acceleration is $6\text{ m/s}^2$.

---

### Example 5.10: Formulating the General $n$-th Derivative Formula
**Problem:** For the reciprocal function $f(x) = x^{-1}$, compute the first four derivatives, discover the pattern, and state the closed-form equation for the $n$-th derivative $f^{(n)}(x)$.

**Step 1:** Calculate first few derivatives:
- $f^{(1)}(x) = -1 x^{-2} = \frac{-1}{x^2}$
- $f^{(2)}(x) = (-1)(-2) x^{-3} = +2 x^{-3} = \frac{2}{x^3}$
- $f^{(3)}(x) = (2)(-3) x^{-4} = -6 x^{-4} = \frac{-6}{x^4}$
- $f^{(4)}(x) = (-6)(-4) x^{-5} = +24 x^{-5} = \frac{24}{x^5}$

**Step 2:** Identify structural patterns:
1. **Signs:** Alternating: $-, +, -, + \dots$ represented by $(-1)^n$.
2. **Coefficients:** $1, 2, 6, 24 \dots$ which are the factorials: $1!, 2!, 3!, 4! \dots = n!$.
3. **Powers of $x$:** $x^{-(n+1)} = \frac{1}{x^{n+1}}$.

**Step 3:** Synthesize the formula:
$$f^{(n)}(x) = (-1)^n \frac{n!}{x^{n+1}}$$

**Step 4:** Verify for $n = 3$:
$$f^{(3)}(x) = (-1)^3 \frac{3!}{x^{3+1}} = -1 \cdot \frac{6}{x^4} = -\frac{6}{x^4}$$
It matches step 1 identically.

---

## 5.8 Complete Step-by-Step Solutions to Chapter Exercises

### Solution to Exercise 5.1
Differentiate $f(x) = 4x^6 - 7x^4 + 9x^2 - 13x + 42$.
Apply the power rule to each term:
- $\frac{d}{dx}[4x^6] = 4(6x^5) = 24x^5$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[-7x^4] = -7(4x^3) = -28x^3$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[9x^2] = 9(2x) = 18x$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[-13x] = -13(1) = -13$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[42] = 0$
$$f'(x) = 24x^5 - 28x^3 + 18x - 13$$
**Final Answer:** $f'(x) = 24x^5 - 28x^3 + 18x - 13$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 5.2
Differentiate $g(t) = \frac{1}{5}t^5 - \frac{4}{3}t^3 + 8t - \pi^2$.
Note that $\pi^2$ is a constant, so its derivative is $0$:
- $\frac{d}{dt}\left[\frac{1}{5}t^5\right] = \frac{1}{5}(5t^4) = t^4$
- $\frac{d}{dt}\left[-\frac{4}{3}t^3\right] = -\frac{4}{3}(3t^2) = -4t^2$
- $\frac{d}{dt}[8t] = 8$
- $\frac{d}{dt}[-\pi^2] = 0$
$$g'(t) = t^4 - 4t^2 + 8$$
**Final Answer:** $g'(t) = t^4 - 4t^2 + 8$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 5.3
Differentiate $y = 8\sqrt{x} - \frac{6}{\sqrt[3]{x}}$.
1. Rewrite in power form:
$$y = 8x^{1/2} - 6x^{-1/3}$$
2. Differentiate:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = 8\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)x^{\frac{1}{2} - 1} - 6\left(-\frac{1}{3}\right)x^{-\frac{1}{3} - 1}$$
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = 4x^{-1/2} + 2x^{-4/3} = \frac{4}{\sqrt{x}} + \frac{2}{\sqrt[3]{x^4}} = \frac{4}{\sqrt{x}} + \frac{2}{x\sqrt[3]{x}}$$
**Final Answer:** $\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{4}{\sqrt{x}} + \frac{2}{x\sqrt[3]{x}}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 5.4
Differentiate $f(u) = 3u^{-3} - 5u^{-1} + 12$.
1. Apply the power rule:
$$f'(u) = 3(-3u^{-4}) - 5(-1u^{-2}) + 0$$
$$f'(u) = -9u^{-4} + 5u^{-2} = -\frac{9}{u^4} + \frac{5}{u^2}$$
**Final Answer:** $f'(u) = -\frac{9}{u^4} + \frac{5}{u^2}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 5.5
Differentiate $h(x) = (3x - 2)(2x^2 + 5x - 1)$ by expanding first.
1. Expand the product:
$$h(x) = 3x(2x^2 + 5x - 1) - 2(2x^2 + 5x - 1)$$
$$h(x) = (6x^3 + 15x^2 - 3x) - (4x^2 + 10x - 2)$$
$$h(x) = 6x^3 + 11x^2 - 13x + 2$$
2. Differentiate term by term:
$$h'(x) = 6(3x^2) + 11(2x) - 13(1) + 0 = 18x^2 + 22x - 13$$
**Final Answer:** $h'(x) = 18x^2 + 22x - 13$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 5.6
Differentiate $R(z) = \frac{9z^4 - 6z^3 + 4z}{2z^2}$.
1. Divide through by the denominator $2z^2$:
$$R(z) = \frac{9z^4}{2z^2} - \frac{6z^3}{2z^2} + \frac{4z}{2z^2} = \frac{9}{2}z^2 - 3z + 2z^{-1}$$
2. Differentiate:
$$R'(z) = \frac{9}{2}(2z) - 3(1) + 2(-1z^{-2}) = 9z - 3 - \frac{2}{z^2}$$
**Final Answer:** $R'(z) = 9z - 3 - \frac{2}{z^2}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 5.7
Find points on $y = \frac{1}{3}x^3 - 4x + 5$ where the tangent is horizontal ($m = 0$).
1. Compute $\frac{dy}{dx}$:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{3}(3x^2) - 4 = x^2 - 4$$
2. Set $\frac{dy}{dx} = 0$:
$$x^2 - 4 = 0 \implies x^2 = 4 \implies x = \pm 2$$
3. Find $y$-coordinates:
- At $x = 2$: $y(2) = \frac{1}{3}(8) - 4(2) + 5 = \frac{8}{3} - 8 + 5 = \frac{8}{3} - 3 = -\frac{1}{3}$.
- At $x = -2$: $y(-2) = \frac{1}{3}(-8) - 4(-2) + 5 = -\frac{8}{3} + 8 + 5 = -\frac{8}{3} + 13 = \frac{31}{3}$.
**Final Answer:** $(2, -\frac{1}{3})$ and $(-2, \frac{31}{3})$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 5.8
Find the normal line to $y = 3x^2 - 8x + 2$ at $x = 1$.
1. Contact point: $y(1) = 3(1)^2 - 8(1) + 2 = 3 - 8 + 2 = -3 \implies P(1, -3)$.
2. Tangent slope: $\frac{dy}{dx} = 6x - 8 \implies m_{\text{tan}} = 6(1) - 8 = -2$.
3. Normal slope: $m_{\text{norm}} = -\frac{1}{m_{\text{tan}}} = -\frac{1}{-2} = \frac{1}{2}$.
4. Normal line equation:
$$y - (-3) = \frac{1}{2}(x - 1) \implies y + 3 = \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{1}{2} \implies y = \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{7}{2}$$
Or in standard form: $x - 2y - 7 = 0$.
**Final Answer:** $y = \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{7}{2}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 5.9
Find the jerk $j(2)$ for $h(t) = -t^4 + 8t^3 - 18t^2 + 50$.
1. Velocity $v(t) = h'(t) = -4t^3 + 24t^2 - 36t$.
2. Acceleration $a(t) = h''(t) = -12t^2 + 48t - 36$.
3. Jerk $j(t) = h'''(t) = -24t + 48$.
4. Evaluate at $t = 2$:
$$j(2) = -24(2) + 48 = -48 + 48 = 0\text{ m/s}^3$$
**Final Answer:** $0\text{ m/s}^3$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 5.10
Prove that for $f(x) = x^{-2}$, $f^{(n)}(x) = (-1)^n \frac{(n+1)!}{x^{n+2}}$.
1. Check base case $n = 1$:
$$f'(x) = -2 x^{-3} = (-1)^1 \frac{2!}{x^{1+2}} = -\frac{2}{x^3}$$
Formula holds for $n = 1$.
2. Compute second derivative ($n = 2$):
$$f''(x) = \frac{d}{dx}[-2x^{-3}] = (-2)(-3)x^{-4} = 6x^{-4} = (-1)^2 \frac{3!}{x^{2+2}} = \frac{6}{x^4}$$
Formula holds for $n = 2$.
3. General step from $k$ to $k+1$:
Assume $f^{(k)}(x) = (-1)^k (k+1)! x^{-(k+2)}$.
Differentiating:
$$f^{(k+1)}(x) = \frac{d}{dx}\left[(-1)^k (k+1)! x^{-(k+2)}\right] = (-1)^k (k+1)! \cdot [-(k+2)] x^{-(k+3)}$$
Notice that $[-(k+2)] = (-1) \cdot (k+2)$:
$$= (-1)^{k+1} \cdot [(k+1)! \cdot (k+2)] \cdot x^{-(k+3)} = (-1)^{k+1} (k+2)! x^{-(k+3)} = (-1)^{k+1} \frac{(k+2)!}{x^{k+3}}$$
By mathematical induction, the formula holds for all integers $n \ge 1$. $\blacksquare$


---\n
# Chapter 6: The Product and Quotient Rules â€” Geometry of Compounding Rates

> *"When two quantities are multiplying together, the rate at which their product expands does not obey naive intuition. The growth of a territory is not just the sum of changes along its borders, but the simultaneous outward push of both length and width creating new area across two dimensions."*

---

## 6.1 The Fallacy of Naive Multiplication

When beginners first encounter the sum rule $\frac{d}{dx}[u + v] = u' + v'$, they frequently jump to the seductive assumption that the derivative of a product must simply be the product of the derivatives:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[u \cdot v] \stackrel{?}{=} u' \cdot v' \quad \mathbf{(CATASTROPHICALLY\ FALSE)}$$

To understand why this is completely wrong, test it on the simplest possible quadratic function:

$$f(x) = x^2 = x \cdot x$$

Let $u(x) = x$ and $v(x) = x$.
We already know with absolute certainty from first principles (Chapter 4) and the power rule (Chapter 5) that:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[x^2] = 2x$$

Now test the naive guess:
$$u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}[x] = 1, \quad v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}[x] = 1$$
$$u'(x) \cdot v'(x) = 1 \cdot 1 = 1$$

If the naive guess were true, the derivative of $x^2$ would be $1$ everywhere â€” meaning a curved parabolic trajectory would have a constant slope of $1$, which is absurd.

Rates of change of products do not multiply; they compound across shared boundaries.

---

## 6.2 The Geometric Proof: The Expanding Rectangle

To see why the correct rule takes the shape it does, imagine a rectangular metal plate being heated in an industrial furnace. 

```
                    u(x)             Î”u
             +--------------------+-------+
             |                    |       |
             |                    |  Î”A_2 |
        v(x) |      Area =        | = vÎ”u |
             |      u(x)Â·v(x)     |       |
             |                    |       |
             +--------------------+-------+
         Î”v  |     Î”A_1 = uÎ”v     | Î”A_3  |
             +--------------------+-------+
```

Let:
- $u(x)$ be the horizontal length of the plate.
- $v(x)$ be the vertical width of the plate.
- $A(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x)$ be the total surface area.

Suppose the control parameter $x$ increases by a small step $h = \Delta x$. As a consequence:
- The length grows from $u$ to $u + \Delta u$.
- The width grows from $v$ to $v + \Delta v$.

The new expanded area is:

$$A_{\text{new}} = (u + \Delta u)(v + \Delta v) = u \cdot v + u \cdot \Delta v + v \cdot \Delta u + \Delta u \cdot \Delta v$$

The net change in area $\Delta A = A_{\text{new}} - A_{\text{initial}}$ consists of three distinct geometric sub-regions:

$$\Delta A = \underbrace{u \cdot \Delta v}_{\text{bottom strip}} + \underbrace{v \cdot \Delta u}_{\text{side strip}} + \underbrace{\Delta u \cdot \Delta v}_{\text{corner patch}}$$

Now divide the entire equation by the step $\Delta x$:

$$\frac{\Delta A}{\Delta x} = u \frac{\Delta v}{\Delta x} + v \frac{\Delta u}{\Delta x} + \Delta u \frac{\Delta v}{\Delta x}$$

Take the limit as $\Delta x \to 0$:
1. $\lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{\Delta v}{\Delta x} = v' = \frac{dv}{dx}$ (the rate of change of width).
2. $\lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{\Delta u}{\Delta x} = u' = \frac{du}{dx}$ (the rate of change of length).
3. Because $u(x)$ is differentiable (and therefore continuous), as $\Delta x \to 0$, the change $\Delta u \to 0$.
4. Look at the tiny corner patch: $\lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \left(\Delta u \frac{\Delta v}{\Delta x}\right) = 0 \cdot v' = 0$.

The corner patch vanishes completely in the limit! We are left with the exact, inviolable **Product Rule**:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[u(x) \cdot v(x)] = u(x) \frac{dv}{dx} + v(x) \frac{du}{dx} = u v' + v u'$$

In words: *"The derivative of a product is the first function times the derivative of the second, plus the second function times the derivative of the first."*

### Verification on $f(x) = x \cdot x$:
$$f'(x) = x \frac{d}{dx}[x] + x \frac{d}{dx}[x] = x(1) + x(1) = 2x$$
The formula delivers the exact truth.

---

## 6.3 The Extended Product Rule for Multiple Factors

What if a system depends on the simultaneous product of three or more interacting subsystems?
For example, in aerodynamics, aerodynamic drag force is modeled as:

$$F_{\text{drag}} = \frac{1}{2} \cdot \rho(h) \cdot v^2(t) \cdot C_D(\alpha)$$

where air density $\rho$, velocity $v$, and drag coefficient $C_D$ all vary dynamically.

For three functions $u(x), v(x), w(x)$, group the first two together: $[u \cdot v] \cdot w$.
Apply the standard product rule:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[(u v) w] = (u v) \frac{dw}{dx} + w \frac{d}{dx}[u v]$$
$$= u v w' + w (u v' + v u') = u v w' + u v' w + u' v w$$

Rearranging symmetrically:

$$\frac{d}{dx}[u \cdot v \cdot w] = u' v w + u v' w + u v w'$$

Each function takes a turn being differentiated while all other factors remain untouched. This pattern extends by mathematical induction to any finite product of $n$ functions:

$$\frac{d}{dx}\left[\prod_{k=1}^n f_k(x)\right] = \sum_{k=1}^n \left( f_k'(x) \prod_{j \neq k} f_j(x) \right)$$

---

## 6.4 The Quotient Rule: Division of Competing Rates

Now consider a ratio of two differentiable functions:

$$f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \quad (\text{where } v(x) \neq 0)$$

### First Principles Limit Derivation
Construct the difference quotient directly:

$$\frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = \frac{\frac{u(x+h)}{v(x+h)} - \frac{u(x)}{v(x)}}{h}$$

Place the numerator fractions over a common denominator $v(x+h) v(x)$:

$$= \frac{u(x+h) v(x) - u(x) v(x+h)}{h \cdot v(x+h) v(x)}$$

Now perform the classic mathematical technique of adding and subtracting the cross-term $u(x) v(x)$ inside the numerator:

$$= \frac{[u(x+h) v(x) - u(x) v(x)] - [u(x) v(x+h) - u(x) v(x)]}{h \cdot v(x+h) v(x)}$$

Factor $v(x)$ from the first group and $u(x)$ from the second group:

$$= \frac{v(x) [u(x+h) - u(x)] - u(x) [v(x+h) - v(x)]}{h \cdot v(x+h) v(x)}$$

Distribute the division by $h$:

$$= \frac{v(x) \left(\frac{u(x+h) - u(x)}{h}\right) - u(x) \left(\frac{v(x+h) - v(x)}{h}\right)}{v(x+h) v(x)}$$

Now take the limit as $h \to 0$:
- $\lim_{h \to 0} \frac{u(x+h) - u(x)}{h} = u'(x)$
- $\lim_{h \to 0} \frac{v(x+h) - v(x)}{h} = v'(x)$
- By continuity of $v$, $\lim_{h \to 0} v(x+h) = v(x)$, so the denominator becomes $v(x) \cdot v(x) = [v(x)]^2$.

We arrive at the universal **Quotient Rule**:

$$\frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{u(x)}{v(x)}\right] = \frac{v(x) u'(x) - u(x) v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{v u' - u v'}{v^2}$$

### The Universal Caution: The Negative Sign
Because addition is commutative, in the product rule $u v' + v u' = v u' + u v'$. Order does not matter.
**In the quotient rule, order is vital.** Subtraction is not commutative:

$$v u' - u v' \neq u v' - v u'$$

Always begin with the denominator multiplying the derivative of the numerator:
$$\mathbf{\frac{(\text{Denominator}) \cdot (\text{Numerator})' - (\text{Numerator}) \cdot (\text{Denominator})'}{(\text{Denominator})^2}}$$

---

## 6.5 Ten Progressive Worked Numerical Examples

### Example 6.1: Differentiating a Product of Two Polynomials
**Problem:** Differentiate $f(x) = (2x^2 - 3)(4x^3 + 5x)$.

**Step 1:** Identify $u(x)$ and $v(x)$:
- $u(x) = 2x^2 - 3 \implies u'(x) = 4x$
- $v(x) = 4x^3 + 5x \implies v'(x) = 12x^2 + 5$

**Step 2:** Apply the product rule:
$$f'(x) = u(x) v'(x) + v(x) u'(x)$$
$$f'(x) = (2x^2 - 3)(12x^2 + 5) + (4x^3 + 5x)(4x)$$

**Step 3:** Expand each product:
$$(2x^2 - 3)(12x^2 + 5) = 24x^4 + 10x^2 - 36x^2 - 15 = 24x^4 - 26x^2 - 15$$
$$(4x^3 + 5x)(4x) = 16x^4 + 20x^2$$

**Step 4:** Combine like terms:
$$f'(x) = (24x^4 - 26x^2 - 15) + (16x^4 + 20x^2) = 40x^4 - 6x^2 - 15$$

**Step 5:** Verification via direct expansion:
$$f(x) = (2x^2 - 3)(4x^3 + 5x) = 8x^5 + 10x^3 - 12x^3 - 15x = 8x^5 - 2x^3 - 15x$$
$$\frac{d}{dx}[8x^5 - 2x^3 - 15x] = 40x^4 - 6x^2 - 15$$
Both methods match identically.

---

### Example 6.2: Product Rule with Radical Factor
**Problem:** Differentiate $g(x) = \sqrt{x}(3x^2 - 8x + 2)$ for $x > 0$.

**Step 1:** Express radicals in power notation:
- $u(x) = x^{1/2} \implies u'(x) = \frac{1}{2}x^{-1/2} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}$
- $v(x) = 3x^2 - 8x + 2 \implies v'(x) = 6x - 8$

**Step 2:** Apply the product rule:
$$g'(x) = u \cdot v' + v \cdot u' = x^{1/2}(6x - 8) + (3x^2 - 8x + 2)\left(\frac{1}{2}x^{-1/2}\right)$$

**Step 3:** Distribute powers:
$$x^{1/2}(6x - 8) = 6x^{3/2} - 8x^{1/2}$$
$$(3x^2 - 8x + 2)\left(\frac{1}{2}x^{-1/2}\right) = \frac{3}{2}x^{3/2} - 4x^{1/2} + x^{-1/2}$$

**Step 4:** Collect like fractional powers:
$$g'(x) = \left(6 + \frac{3}{2}\right)x^{3/2} + (-8 - 4)x^{1/2} + x^{-1/2} = \frac{15}{2}x^{3/2} - 12x^{1/2} + \frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}$$

---

### Example 6.3: Quotient Rule on a Rational Function
**Problem:** Differentiate $y = \frac{3x - 5}{2x + 7}$ for $x \neq -7/2$.

**Step 1:** Identify numerator and denominator:
- Numerator $u = 3x - 5 \implies u' = 3$
- Denominator $v = 2x + 7 \implies v' = 2$

**Step 2:** Set up the quotient rule formula:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{v u' - u v'}{v^2} = \frac{(2x + 7)(3) - (3x - 5)(2)}{(2x + 7)^2}$$

**Step 3:** Expand the numerator carefully:
$$(2x + 7)(3) = 6x + 21$$
$$(3x - 5)(2) = 6x - 10$$
$$(6x + 21) - (6x - 10) = 6x + 21 - 6x + 10 = 31$$

**Step 4:** Write the simplified derivative:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{31}{(2x + 7)^2}$$

Notice that the numerator simplified to a constant, and because the denominator is squared, the derivative is strictly positive for all $x \neq -7/2$. The curve is strictly increasing throughout its domain.

---

### Example 6.4: Rational Function with Quadratic Numerator
**Problem:** Differentiate $f(x) = \frac{x^2 + 4x - 5}{x - 3}$ for $x \neq 3$.

**Step 1:** Define terms:
- $u = x^2 + 4x - 5 \implies u' = 2x + 4$
- $v = x - 3 \implies v' = 1$

**Step 2:** Apply quotient rule:
$$f'(x) = \frac{(x - 3)(2x + 4) - (x^2 + 4x - 5)(1)}{(x - 3)^2}$$

**Step 3:** Expand and simplify the numerator:
$$(x - 3)(2x + 4) = 2x^2 + 4x - 6x - 12 = 2x^2 - 2x - 12$$
$$(x^2 + 4x - 5)(1) = x^2 + 4x - 5$$
$$\text{Numerator} = (2x^2 - 2x - 12) - (x^2 + 4x - 5) = 2x^2 - 2x - 12 - x^2 - 4x + 5$$
$$= x^2 - 6x - 7$$

**Step 4:** Write the derivative:
$$f'(x) = \frac{x^2 - 6x - 7}{(x - 3)^2} = \frac{(x - 7)(x + 1)}{(x - 3)^2}$$

---

### Example 6.5: Dynamic Electrical Power in an AC Subsystem
**Problem:** In an electrical circuit, the time-varying voltage across a load is $V(t) = 120 + 10t$ volts, and the time-varying current is $I(t) = 5 - 0.2t$ amperes. Compute the instantaneous rate of change of power dissipation $\frac{dP}{dt}$ at $t = 10$ seconds.

**Step 1:** Power is the product of voltage and current: $P(t) = V(t) \cdot I(t)$.
By the product rule:
$$\frac{dP}{dt} = V(t) \frac{dI}{dt} + I(t) \frac{dV}{dt}$$

**Step 2:** Compute individual derivatives:
$$\frac{dV}{dt} = \frac{d}{dt}[120 + 10t] = 10\text{ V/s}$$
$$\frac{dI}{dt} = \frac{d}{dt}[5 - 0.2t] = -0.2\text{ A/s}$$

**Step 3:** Evaluate state at $t = 10$ seconds:
$$V(10) = 120 + 10(10) = 120 + 100 = 220\text{ V}$$
$$I(10) = 5 - 0.2(10) = 5 - 2 = 3\text{ A}$$

**Step 4:** Substitute into the product rule:
$$\left.\frac{dP}{dt}\right|_{t=10} = (220\text{ V})(-0.2\text{ A/s}) + (3\text{ A})(10\text{ V/s})$$
$$\frac{dP}{dt} = -44\text{ W/s} + 30\text{ W/s} = -14\text{ W/s}$$

**Conclusion:** At $t = 10\text{ s}$, despite voltage rising at $+10\text{ V/s}$, the declining current causes total power dissipation to decrease at $-14\text{ W/s}$.

---

### Example 6.6: Extended Product Rule for Three Monomial Factors
**Problem:** Differentiate $f(x) = (x - 1)(x + 2)(2x - 3)$ using the 3-term product rule.

**Step 1:** Identify components:
- $u = x - 1 \implies u' = 1$
- $v = x + 2 \implies v' = 1$
- $w = 2x - 3 \implies w' = 2$

**Step 2:** Apply formula $f'(x) = u' v w + u v' w + u v w'$:
$$f'(x) = (1)(x + 2)(2x - 3) + (x - 1)(1)(2x - 3) + (x - 1)(x + 2)(2)$$

**Step 3:** Expand each term:
- Term 1: $(x + 2)(2x - 3) = 2x^2 - 3x + 4x - 6 = 2x^2 + x - 6$
- Term 2: $(x - 1)(2x - 3) = 2x^2 - 3x - 2x + 3 = 2x^2 - 5x + 3$
- Term 3: $2(x - 1)(x + 2) = 2(x^2 + x - 2) = 2x^2 + 2x - 4$

**Step 4:** Sum the three quadratic expressions:
$$f'(x) = (2x^2 + x - 6) + (2x^2 - 5x + 3) + (2x^2 + 2x - 4)$$
$$f'(x) = (2 + 2 + 2)x^2 + (1 - 5 + 2)x + (-6 + 3 - 4) = 6x^2 - 2x - 7$$

---

### Example 6.7: Locating Horizontal Tangent Coordinates on a Rational Curve
**Problem:** Find all points on the graph of $y = \frac{x}{x^2 + 4}$ where the tangent line is horizontal.

**Step 1:** A horizontal tangent occurs when $\frac{dy}{dx} = 0$.

**Step 2:** Differentiate using the quotient rule:
- Numerator $u = x \implies u' = 1$
- Denominator $v = x^2 + 4 \implies v' = 2x$

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{(x^2 + 4)(1) - (x)(2x)}{(x^2 + 4)^2} = \frac{x^2 + 4 - 2x^2}{(x^2 + 4)^2} = \frac{4 - x^2}{(x^2 + 4)^2}$$

**Step 3:** A fraction equals zero if and only if its numerator is zero (and denominator is non-zero):
$$4 - x^2 = 0 \implies x^2 = 4 \implies x = \pm 2$$
(Note that $(x^2 + 4)^2 \ge 16 > 0$ for all real $x$, so no division by zero occurs).

**Step 4:** Calculate $y$-values:
- At $x = 2$: $y(2) = \frac{2}{2^2 + 4} = \frac{2}{4 + 4} = \frac{2}{8} = \frac{1}{4} = 0.25$.
- At $x = -2$: $y(-2) = \frac{-2}{(-2)^2 + 4} = \frac{-2}{4 + 4} = -\frac{2}{8} = -\frac{1}{4} = -0.25$.

**Conclusion:** Horizontal tangents occur at $(2, 0.25)$ (a local maximum) and $(-2, -0.25)$ (a local minimum).

---

### Example 6.8: Normal Line to a Rational Hyperbola
**Problem:** Find the equation of the normal line to the curve $y = \frac{2x}{x + 1}$ at the point $(1, 1)$.

**Step 1:** Verify the contact point:
$$y(1) = \frac{2(1)}{1 + 1} = \frac{2}{2} = 1 \implies P(1, 1)$$

**Step 2:** Compute $\frac{dy}{dx}$ via quotient rule:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{(x + 1)\frac{d}{dx}[2x] - (2x)\frac{d}{dx}[x + 1]}{(x + 1)^2} = \frac{(x + 1)(2) - (2x)(1)}{(x + 1)^2} = \frac{2x + 2 - 2x}{(x + 1)^2} = \frac{2}{(x + 1)^2}$$

**Step 3:** Evaluate tangent slope at $x = 1$:
$$m_{\text{tan}} = \frac{2}{(1 + 1)^2} = \frac{2}{2^2} = \frac{2}{4} = \frac{1}{2}$$

**Step 4:** Determine normal slope:
$$m_{\text{norm}} = -\frac{1}{m_{\text{tan}}} = -\frac{1}{1/2} = -2$$

**Step 5:** Write normal line equation:
$$y - 1 = -2(x - 1) \implies y - 1 = -2x + 2 \implies y = -2x + 3$$
Or in standard form: $2x + y - 3 = 0$.

---

### Example 6.9: Successive Second Derivative of a Rational Function
**Problem:** For $y = \frac{x}{x - 1}$ ($x \neq 1$), find the second derivative $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$.

**Step 1:** Compute the first derivative $\frac{dy}{dx}$:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{(x - 1)(1) - x(1)}{(x - 1)^2} = \frac{x - 1 - x}{(x - 1)^2} = \frac{-1}{(x - 1)^2} = -(x - 1)^{-2}$$

**Step 2:** Compute the second derivative:
$$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{d}{dx}\left[-(x - 1)^{-2}\right]$$
Using the quotient rule on $\frac{-1}{(x - 1)^2}$:
- Numerator $u = -1 \implies u' = 0$
- Denominator $v = (x - 1)^2 = x^2 - 2x + 1 \implies v' = 2x - 2 = 2(x - 1)$

$$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{(x - 1)^2(0) - (-1)[2(x - 1)]}{[(x - 1)^2]^2} = \frac{2(x - 1)}{(x - 1)^4}$$

**Step 3:** Cancel the common factor $(x - 1)$:
$$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{2}{(x - 1)^3}$$

---

### Example 6.10: Rate of Change of Drone Thrust-to-Weight Ratio
**Problem:** A quadcopter drone with variable payload burns fuel during high-altitude climb. Its thrust in newtons is $T(t) = 40 + 2t$, and its total mass in kilograms is $M(t) = 4 - 0.05t$. Calculate the rate of change of the thrust-to-mass ratio $R(t) = \frac{T(t)}{M(t)}$ at $t = 20$ seconds.

**Step 1:** Apply quotient rule to $R(t) = \frac{T(t)}{M(t)}$:
$$R'(t) = \frac{M(t) T'(t) - T(t) M'(t)}{[M(t)]^2}$$

**Step 2:** Compute derivatives:
$$T'(t) = 2\text{ N/s}$$
$$M'(t) = -0.05\text{ kg/s}$$

**Step 3:** Evaluate state at $t = 20\text{ s}$:
$$T(20) = 40 + 2(20) = 80\text{ N}$$
$$M(20) = 4 - 0.05(20) = 4 - 1.0 = 3\text{ kg}$$

**Step 4:** Calculate $R'(20)$:
$$R'(20) = \frac{3(2) - (80)(-0.05)}{3^2} = \frac{6 - (-4.0)}{9} = \frac{6 + 4}{9} = \frac{10}{9} \approx 1.111\text{ N/(kg}\cdot\text{s)}$$

**Conclusion:** At $t = 20\text{ s}$, the thrust-to-mass ratio is accelerating upward at $\frac{10}{9} \approx 1.111\text{ (m/s}^2)/\text{s}$.

---

## 6.7 Complete Step-by-Step Solutions to Chapter Exercises

### Solution to Exercise 6.1
Differentiate $f(x) = (3x - 4)(5x + 2)$.
1. $u = 3x - 4 \implies u' = 3$.
2. $v = 5x + 2 \implies v' = 5$.
3. Product rule:
$$f'(x) = u v' + v u' = (3x - 4)(5) + (5x + 2)(3)$$
$$f'(x) = (15x - 20) + (15x + 6) = 30x - 14$$
**Final Answer:** $f'(x) = 30x - 14$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 6.2
Differentiate $g(x) = (x^2 + 1)(2x^3 - 4x)$.
1. $u = x^2 + 1 \implies u' = 2x$.
2. $v = 2x^3 - 4x \implies v' = 6x^2 - 4$.
3. Product rule:
$$g'(x) = (x^2 + 1)(6x^2 - 4) + (2x^3 - 4x)(2x)$$
$$= (6x^4 - 4x^2 + 6x^2 - 4) + (4x^4 - 8x^2)$$
$$= (6x^4 + 2x^2 - 4) + (4x^4 - 8x^2) = 10x^4 - 6x^2 - 4$$
**Final Answer:** $g'(x) = 10x^4 - 6x^2 - 4$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 6.3
Differentiate $y = \frac{4x + 1}{3x - 2}$.
1. $u = 4x + 1 \implies u' = 4$.
2. $v = 3x - 2 \implies v' = 3$.
3. Quotient rule:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{(3x - 2)(4) - (4x + 1)(3)}{(3x - 2)^2}$$
$$= \frac{12x - 8 - (12x + 3)}{(3x - 2)^2} = \frac{12x - 8 - 12x - 3}{(3x - 2)^2} = \frac{-11}{(3x - 2)^2}$$
**Final Answer:** $\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{11}{(3x - 2)^2}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 6.4
Differentiate $f(t) = \frac{t^2}{2t + 5}$.
1. $u = t^2 \implies u' = 2t$.
2. $v = 2t + 5 \implies v' = 2$.
3. Quotient rule:
$$f'(t) = \frac{(2t + 5)(2t) - (t^2)(2)}{(2t + 5)^2} = \frac{4t^2 + 10t - 2t^2}{(2t + 5)^2} = \frac{2t^2 + 10t}{(2t + 5)^2} = \frac{2t(t + 5)}{(2t + 5)^2}$$
**Final Answer:** $f'(t) = \frac{2t(t + 5)}{(2t + 5)^2}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 6.5
Differentiate $h(x) = \frac{\sqrt{x}}{x^2 + 1}$.
1. $u = x^{1/2} \implies u' = \frac{1}{2}x^{-1/2} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}$.
2. $v = x^2 + 1 \implies v' = 2x$.
3. Quotient rule:
$$h'(x) = \frac{(x^2 + 1)\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}\right) - \sqrt{x}(2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}$$
Multiply numerator and denominator by $2\sqrt{x}$:
$$h'(x) = \frac{(x^2 + 1) - 2x \cdot (2x)}{2\sqrt{x}(x^2 + 1)^2} = \frac{x^2 + 1 - 4x^2}{2\sqrt{x}(x^2 + 1)^2} = \frac{1 - 3x^2}{2\sqrt{x}(x^2 + 1)^2}$$
**Final Answer:** $h'(x) = \frac{1 - 3x^2}{2\sqrt{x}(x^2 + 1)^2}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 6.6
Differentiate $y = (x^3 - 2x)(x^{-2} + 4)$.
1. Expand before differentiating (or use product rule):
$$y = x^3(x^{-2}) + 4x^3 - 2x(x^{-2}) - 8x = x + 4x^3 - 2x^{-1} - 8x = 4x^3 - 7x - 2x^{-1}$$
2. Differentiate term by term:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = 12x^2 - 7 - 2(-1x^{-2}) = 12x^2 - 7 + \frac{2}{x^2}$$
**Final Answer:** $\frac{dy}{dx} = 12x^2 - 7 + \frac{2}{x^2}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 6.7
Find tangent line to $y = \frac{x - 1}{x + 1}$ at $x = 3$.
1. Contact point: $y(3) = \frac{3 - 1}{3 + 1} = \frac{2}{4} = \frac{1}{2} \implies P(3, 0.5)$.
2. Derivative via quotient rule:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{(x + 1)(1) - (x - 1)(1)}{(x + 1)^2} = \frac{x + 1 - x + 1}{(x + 1)^2} = \frac{2}{(x + 1)^2}$$
3. Slope at $x = 3$:
$$m = \frac{2}{(3 + 1)^2} = \frac{2}{16} = \frac{1}{8}$$
4. Tangent line:
$$y - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{8}(x - 3) \implies y = \frac{1}{8}x - \frac{3}{8} + \frac{4}{8} \implies y = \frac{1}{8}x + \frac{1}{8}$$
Or in standard form: $x - 8y + 1 = 0$.
**Final Answer:** $y = \frac{1}{8}x + \frac{1}{8}$ (or $x - 8y + 1 = 0$).

---

### Solution to Exercise 6.8
Find horizontal tangents for $y = \frac{x^2}{x - 2}$ ($x \neq 2$).
1. Compute $\frac{dy}{dx}$:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{(x - 2)(2x) - (x^2)(1)}{(x - 2)^2} = \frac{2x^2 - 4x - x^2}{(x - 2)^2} = \frac{x^2 - 4x}{(x - 2)^2} = \frac{x(x - 4)}{(x - 2)^2}$$
2. Set $\frac{dy}{dx} = 0 \implies x(x - 4) = 0 \implies x = 0$ or $x = 4$.
3. Compute coordinates:
- At $x = 0$: $y(0) = \frac{0}{-2} = 0 \implies (0, 0)$.
- At $x = 4$: $y(4) = \frac{4^2}{4 - 2} = \frac{16}{2} = 8 \implies (4, 8)$.
**Final Answer:** $(0, 0)$ and $(4, 8)$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 6.9
Differentiate $P(x) = (x + 1)(x + 2)(x + 3)$.
1. Identify components: $u = x+1 (u'=1)$, $v = x+2 (v'=1)$, $w = x+3 (w'=1)$.
2. Apply formula:
$$P'(x) = (1)(x+2)(x+3) + (x+1)(1)(x+3) + (x+1)(x+2)(1)$$
3. Expand:
$$(x^2 + 5x + 6) + (x^2 + 4x + 3) + (x^2 + 3x + 2) = 3x^2 + 12x + 11$$
**Final Answer:** $P'(x) = 3x^2 + 12x + 11$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 6.10
Evaluate $S'(6)$ for $S(t) = \frac{100 - t}{t^2 + 4}$.
1. Apply quotient rule:
- $u = 100 - t \implies u' = -1$
- $v = t^2 + 4 \implies v' = 2t$
$$S'(t) = \frac{(t^2 + 4)(-1) - (100 - t)(2t)}{(t^2 + 4)^2} = \frac{-t^2 - 4 - 200t + 2t^2}{(t^2 + 4)^2} = \frac{t^2 - 200t - 4}{(t^2 + 4)^2}$$
2. Substitute $t = 6$:
- Numerator: $6^2 - 200(6) - 4 = 36 - 1200 - 4 = -1168$.
- Denominator: $(6^2 + 4)^2 = (36 + 4)^2 = 40^2 = 1600$.
$$S'(6) = \frac{-1168}{1600} = -\frac{73}{100} = -0.73$$
**Final Answer:** $S'(6) = -0.73$.
