## 8.9 Complete Step-by-Step Solutions to Chapter Exercises

### Solution to Exercise 8.1
Differentiate $f(x) = 3\sin x - 4\cos x + 5\tan x$.
Differentiate term by term:
- $\frac{d}{dx}[3\sin x] = 3\cos x$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[-4\cos x] = -4(-\sin x) = +4\sin x$
- $\frac{d}{dx}[5\tan x] = 5\sec^2 x$
$$f'(x) = 3\cos x + 4\sin x + 5\sec^2 x$$
**Final Answer:** $f'(x) = 3\cos x + 4\sin x + 5\sec^2 x$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 8.2
Differentiate $g(\theta) = 2\theta^2 - \cot\theta$.
- $\frac{d}{d\theta}[2\theta^2] = 4\theta$
- $\frac{d}{d\theta}[-\cot\theta] = -(-\csc^2\theta) = +\csc^2\theta$
$$g'(\theta) = 4\theta + \csc^2\theta$$
**Final Answer:** $g'(\theta) = 4\theta + \csc^2\theta$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 8.3
Differentiate $y = x^2 \sin x$.
Apply the product rule: $u = x^2 (u'=2x)$, $v = \sin x (v'=\cos x)$.
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = x^2 \cos x + \sin x (2x) = x^2 \cos x + 2x \sin x = x(x\cos x + 2\sin x)$$
**Final Answer:** $\frac{dy}{dx} = x^2 \cos x + 2x \sin x$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 8.4
Differentiate $f(x) = \frac{\cos x}{x + 1}$.
Apply the quotient rule:
- Numerator $u = \cos x \implies u' = -\sin x$
- Denominator $v = x + 1 \implies v' = 1$
$$f'(x) = \frac{(x + 1)(-\sin x) - (\cos x)(1)}{(x + 1)^2} = \frac{-(x + 1)\sin x - \cos x}{(x + 1)^2}$$
**Final Answer:** $f'(x) = -\frac{(x + 1)\sin x + \cos x}{(x + 1)^2}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 8.5
Differentiate $y = \cos(7x^2 - 3)$.
Apply the chain rule:
$$\frac{dy}{dx} = -\sin(7x^2 - 3) \cdot \frac{d}{dx}[7x^2 - 3] = -\sin(7x^2 - 3) \cdot (14x) = -14x \sin(7x^2 - 3)$$
**Final Answer:** $\frac{dy}{dx} = -14x \sin(7x^2 - 3)$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 8.6
Differentiate $h(t) = \tan^3(4t) = [\tan(4t)]^3$.
Apply multi-layer chain rule:
$$h'(t) = 3[\tan(4t)]^2 \cdot \frac{d}{dt}[\tan(4t)] = 3\tan^2(4t) \cdot [\sec^2(4t) \cdot 4] = 12\tan^2(4t)\sec^2(4t)$$
**Final Answer:** $h'(t) = 12\tan^2(4t)\sec^2(4t)$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 8.7
Find tangent line to $y = \sin(2x)$ at $x_0 = \frac{\pi}{6}$.
1. Find $y_0$:
$$y_0 = \sin\left(2 \cdot \frac{\pi}{6}\right) = \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \implies P\left(\frac{\pi}{6}, \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)$$
2. Derivative:
$$y' = \cos(2x) \cdot 2 = 2\cos(2x)$$
3. Slope at $x = \frac{\pi}{6}$:
$$m = 2\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = 2\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = 1$$
4. Tangent line:
$$y - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = 1\left(x - \frac{\pi}{6}\right) \implies y = x - \frac{\pi}{6} + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$$
**Final Answer:** $y = x - \frac{\pi}{6} + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 8.8
Find normal line to $y = \cos x$ at $x_0 = \frac{\pi}{2}$.
1. Find $y_0$:
$$y_0 = \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 0 \implies P\left(\frac{\pi}{2}, 0\right)$$
2. Tangent slope:
$$y' = -\sin x \implies m_{\text{tan}} = -\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = -1$$
3. Normal slope:
$$m_{\text{norm}} = -\frac{1}{m_{\text{tan}}} = -\frac{1}{-1} = +1$$
4. Normal line equation:
$$y - 0 = 1\left(x - \frac{\pi}{2}\right) \implies y = x - \frac{\pi}{2}$$
**Final Answer:** $y = x - \frac{\pi}{2}$ (or $2x - 2y - \pi = 0$).

---

### Solution to Exercise 8.9
Verify that $y(t) = 3\cos(5t) - 4\sin(5t)$ satisfies $y'' + 25y = 0$.
1. First derivative:
$$y'(t) = 3(-5\sin(5t)) - 4(5\cos(5t)) = -15\sin(5t) - 20\cos(5t)$$
2. Second derivative:
$$y''(t) = -15(5\cos(5t)) - 20(-5\sin(5t)) = -75\cos(5t) + 100\sin(5t)$$
3. Substitute into $y'' + 25y$:
$$y'' + 25y = [-75\cos(5t) + 100\sin(5t)] + 25[3\cos(5t) - 4\sin(5t)]$$
$$= -75\cos(5t) + 100\sin(5t) + 75\cos(5t) - 100\sin(5t) = 0 \quad \blacksquare$$
**Final Answer:** Verified. $y'' + 25y \equiv 0$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 8.10
Evaluate $\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(6x)}{\sin(2x)}$.
1. Rewrite using fundamental limits by multiplying and dividing by $6x$ and $2x$:
$$\frac{\sin(6x)}{\sin(2x)} = \frac{\frac{\sin(6x)}{6x} \cdot 6x}{\frac{\sin(2x)}{2x} \cdot 2x} = \frac{\frac{\sin(6x)}{6x}}{\frac{\sin(2x)}{2x}} \cdot \frac{6x}{2x}$$
2. For $x \neq 0$, $\frac{6x}{2x} = 3$.
3. Take the limit as $x \to 0$:
- $\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(6x)}{6x} = 1$
- $\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(2x)}{2x} = 1$
$$= \frac{1}{1} \cdot 3 = 3$$
**Final Answer:** $3$.


---\n
# Chapter 9: The Physical Hierarchy of Motion â€” Position, Velocity, Acceleration, Jerk, and Snap

> *"To the human eye, an elevator that suddenly starts moving feels uncomfortable, but one that starts with an instantaneous jerk can cause physical injury. Physics and engineering cannot stop at acceleration; they must climb the infinite ladder of time derivatives to tame forces, mechanical wear, and physiological comfort."*

---

## 9.1 The Kinematic Ladder

In physics simulation and classical mechanics, differential calculus is the native language of motion. If we record the position of a body in space as a function of time $t$, each successive derivative reveals a deeper layer of physical reality:

| Derivative Order | Mathematical Notation | Common Physical Name | Metric Units (SI) | Physical Significance |
| :---: | :---: | :--- | :---: | :--- |
| **0** | $s(t)$ or $\vec{r}(t)$ | **Position (Displacement)** | $\text{m}$ | Where the object is located in space. |
| **1** | $v(t) = \dot{s} = \frac{ds}{dt}$ | **Velocity** | $\text{m/s}$ | Rate at which position changes; direction of motion. |
| **2** | $a(t) = \ddot{s} = \frac{dv}{dt} = \frac{d^2s}{dt^2}$ | **Acceleration** | $\text{m/s}^2$ | Rate at which velocity changes; proportional to Net Force ($F = ma$). |
| **3** | $j(t) = \dddot{s} = \frac{da}{dt} = \frac{d^3s}{dt^3}$ | **Jerk (Jolt)** | $\text{m/s}^3$ | Rate at which acceleration changes; rate of change of force on human passengers and structures. |
| **4** | $s_4(t) = s^{(4)} = \frac{dj}{dt} = \frac{d^4s}{dt^4}$ | **Snap (Jounce)** | $\text{m/s}^4$ | Rate of change of jerk; critical in aerospace guidance, drone gimbal paths, and CNC tool wear. |
| **5** | $c(t) = s^{(5)} = \frac{ds_4}{dt}$ | **Crackle** | $\text{m/s}^5$ | Higher-order trajectory smoothing. |
| **6** | $p(t) = s^{(6)} = \frac{dc}{dt}$ | **Pop** | $\text{m/s}^6$ | Precision trajectory smoothing. |

In this chapter, we will master the interplay among these derivatives and formulate the mathematical criteria governing realistic, smooth physical motion in simulation and engineering.

---

## 9.2 Velocity vs. Speed: The Critical Distinction

In common conversation, people use "speed" and "velocity" interchangeably. In mathematics and physics, they are fundamentally different:

1. **Velocity ($v(t)$)** is a **signed scalar** in one dimension (or a vector in multiple dimensions).
   - $v(t) > 0$ means the body is moving in the **positive direction** (e.g., rightward, upward, forward).
   - $v(t) < 0$ means the body is moving in the **negative direction** (e.g., leftward, downward, reverse).
   - $v(t) = 0$ means the body is **momentarily at rest** (stationary or at a turning point).

2. **Speed ($|v(t)|$)** is the **absolute magnitude** of velocity:
   $$\text{Speed} = |v(t)| = \left|\frac{ds}{dt}\right| \ge 0$$
   Speed is never negative. A car traveling in reverse at $-25\text{ m/s}$ has a speed of $+25\text{ m/s}$.

---

## 9.3 The Diagnostic Test: Speeding Up vs. Slowing Down

One of the most persistent misconceptions among engineering and physics students is that:
- *"Positive acceleration always means speeding up."* $\mathbf{(FALSE)}$
- *"Negative acceleration always means slowing down."* $\mathbf{(FALSE)}$

To see why this is false, consider a vehicle moving in reverse with velocity $v = -20\text{ m/s}$. If the driver presses the accelerator in reverse, the acceleration is $a = -4\text{ m/s}^2$. 
One second later, the velocity becomes:
$$v = -20 + (-4)(1) = -24\text{ m/s}$$
The speed was $20\text{ m/s}$; now the speed is $24\text{ m/s}$. The vehicle **sped up**, even though the acceleration was strictly negative!

### The Exact Mathematical Criterion
Consider the rate of change of speed squared ($v^2$):

$$\frac{d}{dt}\left[ (v(t))^2 \right] = 2 v(t) \cdot \frac{dv}{dt} = 2 v(t) a(t)$$

Since speed is $|v| = \sqrt{v^2}$, speed is strictly increasing whenever $v^2$ is increasing:

$$\mathbf{v(t) \cdot a(t) > 0 \iff \text{The body is SPEEDING UP}}$$
$$\mathbf{v(t) \cdot a(t) < 0 \iff \text{The body is SLOWING DOWN}}$$

```
                       Velocity > 0                   Velocity < 0
               (Moving Right / Forward)        (Moving Left / Reverse)
             +------------------------------+------------------------------+
Acceleration |       v > 0, a > 0           |         v < 0, a > 0         |
    > 0      |        SPEEDING UP           |         SLOWING DOWN         |
             +------------------------------+------------------------------+
Acceleration |       v > 0, a < 0           |         v < 0, a < 0         |
    < 0      |        SLOWING DOWN          |         SPEEDING UP          |
             +------------------------------+------------------------------+
```

Summary rule:
- **Same signs** ($v$ and $a$ both positive, or both negative) $\implies$ **SPEEDING UP**.
- **Opposite signs** ($v$ positive and $a$ negative, or vice versa) $\implies$ **SLOWING DOWN**.

---

## 9.4 Why Jerk and Snap Matter in the Physical World

According to Newton's Second Law:

$$F_{\text{net}} = m \cdot a$$

If acceleration $a(t)$ changes discontinuously (for example, stepping instantly from $0\text{ m/s}^2$ to $3\text{ m/s}^2$), the force applied to the object must also jump discontinuously.
Differentiating Newton's law with respect to time (assuming constant mass $m$):

$$\frac{dF}{dt} = m \cdot \frac{da}{dt} = m \cdot j(t)$$

The time derivative of force is directly proportional to **jerk**.

### Physiological Impact on Humans
The human vestibular system (the semicircular canals and otoliths in the inner ear) detects acceleration. When acceleration is constant, our muscles can contract to maintain posture. But when acceleration changes rapidly (high jerk), our muscles cannot adjust instantaneously, causing the head to snap backward or forward (whiplash) and provoking acute motion sickness.
- **Passenger Elevators:** International safety codes (ISO 18738) limit passenger elevator jerk to $|j| \le 1.0\text{ to }1.5\text{ m/s}^3$.
- **High-Speed Rail:** Railway transitions use clothoid curves so centrifugal acceleration ramps linearly rather than abruptly.
- **Drone Gimbals and Robotics:** Infinite jerk (step changes in acceleration) causes mechanical shudder, gears to strip, and camera footage to vibrate. High-end motion planners use **S-curve trajectories** that enforce continuous acceleration with bounded snap.

```
       Acceleration Profile                 Jerk Profile (d/dt of a)
       a ^                                  j ^
         |      .-------.                     |   +---+
         |     /         \                    |   |   |
         |    /           \                   |---+   +---+-------+---> t
         |   /             \                  |               |   |
         +--+---------------+---> t           |               +---+
            Ramp-up  Ramp-down                Finite Jerk (Comfortable)
```

---

## 9.5 Ten Progressive Worked Numerical Examples

### Example 9.1: Comprehensive Motion Diagnostic
**Problem:** A robotic test vehicle moves along a straight rail. Its position in meters over time $t \ge 0$ is given by:
$$s(t) = t^3 - 6t^2 + 9t + 4$$
1. Find formulas for velocity $v(t)$, acceleration $a(t)$, and jerk $j(t)$.
2. Determine all times when the vehicle is momentarily at rest.
3. Determine all intervals when the vehicle is moving forward vs backward.
4. Calculate the position, velocity, and acceleration at $t = 4$ seconds.

**Step 1:** Successive differentiation:
$$v(t) = s'(t) = 3t^2 - 12t + 9$$
$$a(t) = v'(t) = 6t - 12$$
$$j(t) = a'(t) = 6\text{ m/s}^3 \quad (\text{constant jerk})$$

**Step 2:** Find when the vehicle is at rest ($v(t) = 0$):
$$3t^2 - 12t + 9 = 0 \implies 3(t^2 - 4t + 3) = 0 \implies 3(t - 1)(t - 3) = 0$$
The vehicle is momentarily at rest at $t = 1\text{ s}$ and $t = 3\text{ s}$.

**Step 3:** Direction of motion:
- For $t \in [0, 1)$: $v(0.5) = 3(0.5 - 1)(0.5 - 3) = 3(-0.5)(-2.5) = +3.75 > 0$ $\implies$ Moving **forward**.
- For $t \in (1, 3)$: $v(2) = 3(2 - 1)(2 - 3) = 3(1)(-1) = -3 < 0$ $\implies$ Moving **backward**.
- For $t \in (3, \infty)$: $v(4) = 3(4 - 1)(4 - 3) = 3(3)(1) = +9 > 0$ $\implies$ Moving **forward**.

**Step 4:** Evaluate state at $t = 4\text{ s}$:
$$s(4) = 4^3 - 6(4^2) + 9(4) + 4 = 64 - 96 + 36 + 4 = 8\text{ m}$$
$$v(4) = 3(4^2) - 12(4) + 9 = 48 - 48 + 9 = 9\text{ m/s}$$
$$a(4) = 6(4) - 12 = 24 - 12 = 12\text{ m/s}^2$$

---

### Example 9.2: Speeding Up vs Slowing Down Interval Analysis
**Problem:** For the robotic vehicle in Example 9.1 ($s(t) = t^3 - 6t^2 + 9t + 4$), determine the exact time intervals when the vehicle is **speeding up** and when it is **slowing down**.

**Step 1:** List the critical zeros of $v(t)$ and $a(t)$:
- $v(t) = 3(t - 1)(t - 3) = 0 \implies t = 1, t = 3$
- $a(t) = 6(t - 2) = 0 \implies t = 2$

These three points divide the time domain $t \ge 0$ into four intervals:
1. $t \in (0, 1)$
2. $t \in (1, 2)$
3. $t \in (2, 3)$
4. $t \in (3, \infty)$

**Step 2:** Test the signs in each interval:

| Interval | Test $t$ | $v(t)$ Sign | $a(t)$ Sign | $v(t) \cdot a(t)$ Product | Motion State |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :--- |
| **$(0, 1)$** | $t = 0.5$ | $v(0.5) > 0$ | $a(0.5) = 6(0.5) - 12 = -9 < 0$ | **Negative** ($-$) | **SLOWING DOWN** |
| **$(1, 2)$** | $t = 1.5$ | $v(1.5) < 0$ | $a(1.5) = 6(1.5) - 12 = -3 < 0$ | **Positive** ($+$) | **SPEEDING UP** |
| **$(2, 3)$** | $t = 2.5$ | $v(2.5) < 0$ | $a(2.5) = 6(2.5) - 12 = +3 > 0$ | **Negative** ($-$) | **SLOWING DOWN** |
| **$(3, \infty)$** | $t = 4.0$ | $v(4) > 0$ | $a(4) = 6(4) - 12 = +12 > 0$ | **Positive** ($+$) | **SPEEDING UP** |

**Conclusion:**
- The vehicle is **speeding up** on $t \in (1, 2) \cup (3, \infty)$.
- The vehicle is **slowing down** on $t \in (0, 1) \cup (2, 3)$.

---

### Example 9.3: Maglev S-Curve Jerk and Snap Limits
**Problem:** A magnetic levitation train starts from rest at $t = 0$ along a trajectory programmed by $s(t) = \frac{1}{20}t^5 - \frac{1}{4}t^4 + \frac{1}{3}t^3$ meters for $0 \le t \le 3$.
1. Compute the jerk $j(t)$ and snap $s_4(t)$.
2. Find the maximum jerk experienced by the passengers during this interval.

**Step 1:** Differentiate successively:
$$v(t) = s'(t) = \frac{5}{20}t^4 - t^3 + t^2 = \frac{1}{4}t^4 - t^3 + t^2$$
$$a(t) = v'(t) = t^3 - 3t^2 + 2t$$
$$j(t) = a'(t) = 3t^2 - 6t + 2$$
$$s_4(t) = j'(t) = 6t - 6$$

**Step 2:** Find the critical points of jerk $j(t)$:
Set $j'(t) = s_4(t) = 0$:
$$6t - 6 = 0 \implies t = 1\text{ s}$$

**Step 3:** Evaluate jerk at the critical point and boundary endpoints ($t = 0, 1, 3$):
- At $t = 0$: $j(0) = 3(0)^2 - 6(0) + 2 = +2.0\text{ m/s}^3$
- At $t = 1$: $j(1) = 3(1)^2 - 6(1) + 2 = 3 - 6 + 2 = -1.0\text{ m/s}^3$
- At $t = 3$: $j(3) = 3(3^2) - 6(3) + 2 = 27 - 18 + 2 = +11.0\text{ m/s}^3$

**Step 4:** Determine maximum jerk magnitude:
The maximum magnitude is $|j(3)| = 11.0\text{ m/s}^3$.

---

### Example 9.4: Sounding Rocket Apogee and Acceleration
**Problem:** A weather research rocket is launched vertically. Its altitude in meters while the booster burns is modeled by:
$$y(t) = 150t + 20t^2 - \frac{1}{3}t^3 \quad (\text{for } 0 \le t \le 30\text{ s})$$
1. Determine its velocity function $v(t)$ and acceleration function $a(t)$.
2. At what instant does the rocket achieve its maximum velocity?
3. What is that maximum velocity?

**Step 1:** Successive derivatives:
$$v(t) = y'(t) = 150 + 40t - t^2$$
$$a(t) = v'(t) = 40 - 2t$$

**Step 2:** Maximum velocity occurs when the rate of change of velocity (acceleration) is zero:
$$a(t) = 0 \implies 40 - 2t = 0 \implies 2t = 40 \implies t = 20\text{ s}$$

**Step 3:** Confirm maximum using the second derivative test:
$$v''(t) = a'(t) = -2 < 0 \implies \text{Concave down (Maximum verified)}$$

**Step 4:** Calculate maximum velocity at $t = 20\text{ s}$:
$$v(20) = 150 + 40(20) - (20)^2 = 150 + 800 - 400 = 550\text{ m/s}$$

**Conclusion:** The rocket achieves its peak velocity of $550\text{ m/s}$ (Mach $1.6$) at $t = 20$ seconds.

---

### Example 9.5: Drone Camera Gimbal Snap Evaluation
**Problem:** A quadcopter drone executes an evasive tilt maneuver with pitch angle $\theta(t) = 2t^5 - 5t^4 + 10$ radians for $t \in [0, 2]$. Calculate the pitch snap $\theta^{(4)}(t)$ at $t = 1.5$ seconds.

**Step 1:** Differentiate four times:
- 1st: $\theta'(t) = 10t^4 - 20t^3$
- 2nd: $\theta''(t) = 40t^3 - 60t^2$
- 3rd: $\theta'''(t) = 120t^2 - 120t$
- 4th (Snap): $\theta^{(4)}(t) = 240t - 120$

**Step 2:** Evaluate at $t = 1.5$ seconds:
$$\theta^{(4)}(1.5) = 240(1.5) - 120 = 360 - 120 = 240\text{ rad/s}^4$$

---

### Example 9.6: Deceleration and Stopping Distance of an Autonomous Haulage Truck
**Problem:** An autonomous mining truck traveling at $v_0 = 30\text{ m/s}$ detects an obstacle and engages continuous emergency braking such that its position from brake onset is:
$$s(t) = 30t - 2.5t^2$$
1. How long does it take for the truck to come to a complete stop?
2. What is the total stopping distance?
3. What is the constant deceleration rate?

**Step 1:** Compute velocity:
$$v(t) = s'(t) = 30 - 5t$$

**Step 2:** The truck comes to a stop when $v(t) = 0$:
$$30 - 5t = 0 \implies 5t = 30 \implies t = 6\text{ seconds}$$

**Step 3:** Compute stopping distance by evaluating $s(6)$:
$$s(6) = 30(6) - 2.5(6^2) = 180 - 2.5(36) = 180 - 90 = 90\text{ meters}$$

**Step 4:** Compute acceleration:
$$a(t) = v'(t) = -5\text{ m/s}^2$$
The truck decelerates at a constant rate of $5\text{ m/s}^2$ ($0.51 g$).

---

### Example 9.7: Higher Derivatives of Harmonic Simple Motion
**Problem:** A vibrating beam sensor measures displacement $x(t) = 4\cos(3t)$ mm. Determine formulas for $v(t), a(t), j(t)$, and express $a(t)$ and $j(t)$ directly in terms of position $x(t)$ and velocity $v(t)$.

**Step 1:** Compute derivatives:
- $v(t) = x'(t) = 4(-\sin(3t) \cdot 3) = -12\sin(3t)$
- $a(t) = v'(t) = -12(\cos(3t) \cdot 3) = -36\cos(3t)$
- $j(t) = a'(t) = -36(-\sin(3t) \cdot 3) = +108\sin(3t)$

**Step 2:** Express in terms of $x(t)$ and $v(t)$:
Notice that $a(t) = -36\cos(3t) = -9 \cdot [4\cos(3t)] = -9 x(t) = -\omega^2 x(t)$ (with $\omega = 3$).
Notice that $j(t) = 108\sin(3t) = -9 \cdot [-12\sin(3t)] = -9 v(t) = -\omega^2 v(t)$.

**Conclusion:** For harmonic motion of frequency $\omega$, acceleration is $-\omega^2 \cdot \text{position}$, and jerk is $-\omega^2 \cdot \text{velocity}$!

---

### Example 9.8: Reversing Direction and Turning Points
**Problem:** A particle moves along a straight coordinate axis with $s(t) = \frac{1}{3}t^3 - 4t^2 + 12t$ meters for $t \ge 0$.
1. Find all instants when the particle changes its direction of motion.
2. Find the positions of these turning points.

**Step 1:** Direction changes occur when velocity $v(t)$ passes through zero and changes sign.
$$v(t) = s'(t) = t^2 - 8t + 12 = (t - 2)(t - 6)$$

**Step 2:** Set $v(t) = 0 \implies t = 2\text{ s}$ and $t = 6\text{ s}$.

**Step 3:** Verify sign changes:
- For $t < 2$: $(t-2)(t-6) = (-)(-) = + \implies v > 0$ (Moving right)
- For $2 < t < 6$: $(+)(-) = - \implies v < 0$ (Moving left)
- For $t > 6$: $(+)(+) = + \implies v > 0$ (Moving right)
Both $t = 2$ and $t = 6$ are genuine turning points where direction reverses!

**Step 4:** Evaluate positions:
- At $t = 2$: $s(2) = \frac{1}{3}(8) - 4(4) + 12(2) = \frac{8}{3} - 16 + 24 = \frac{8}{3} + 8 = \frac{32}{3} \approx 10.67\text{ m}$.
- At $t = 6$: $s(6) = \frac{1}{3}(216) - 4(36) + 12(6) = 72 - 144 + 72 = 0\text{ m}$.

---

### Example 9.9: High-Speed Passenger Elevator Safety Verification
**Problem:** A skyscraper elevator ascends from ground level to the observation deck according to the velocity profile:
$$v(t) = \begin{cases} 0.5t^2, & 0 \le t \le 2 \\ 2.0, & 2 < t \le 18 \\ 2.0 - 0.25(t - 18)^2, & 18 < t \le 20 \end{cases}$$
in meters per second. Calculate the jerk at $t = 1.0\text{ s}$ and verify whether it exceeds the comfort limit of $1.5\text{ m/s}^3$.

**Step 1:** In the launch phase ($0 \le t \le 2$), $v(t) = 0.5t^2$.
**Step 2:** Acceleration:
$$a(t) = v'(t) = \frac{d}{dt}[0.5t^2] = 1.0t\text{ m/s}^2$$
**Step 3:** Jerk:
$$j(t) = a'(t) = \frac{d}{dt}[1.0t] = 1.0\text{ m/s}^3$$
**Step 4:** Verify safety threshold:
At $t = 1.0\text{ s}$, $j(1.0) = 1.0\text{ m/s}^3$.
Since $1.0\text{ m/s}^3 \le 1.5\text{ m/s}^3$, the motion is well within passenger comfort standards.

---

### Example 9.10: 2D Parametric Vector Kinematics
**Problem:** In a 2D physics simulation, a drone's horizontal and vertical coordinates evolve as:
$$\vec{r}(t) = \langle x(t), y(t) \rangle = \langle 3t^2 - 4t, \ 2t^3 - 6t \rangle\text{ meters}$$
1. Find the velocity vector $\vec{v}(t)$ and acceleration vector $\vec{a}(t)$ at $t = 2$ seconds.
2. Calculate the scalar speed $|\vec{v}(2)|$ at $t = 2$ seconds.

**Step 1:** Differentiate each component independently with respect to time:
$$\vec{v}(t) = \langle x'(t), y'(t) \rangle = \langle 6t - 4, \ 6t^2 - 6 \rangle$$
$$\vec{a}(t) = \langle x''(t), y''(t) \rangle = \langle 6, \ 12t \rangle$$

**Step 2:** Evaluate at $t = 2\text{ s}$:
$$\vec{v}(2) = \langle 6(2) - 4, \ 6(2^2) - 6 \rangle = \langle 12 - 4, \ 24 - 6 \rangle = \langle 8, 18 \rangle\text{ m/s}$$
$$\vec{a}(2) = \langle 6, \ 12(2) \rangle = \langle 6, 24 \rangle\text{ m/s}^2$$

**Step 3:** Compute scalar speed $|\vec{v}(2)|$ using the Pythagorean theorem:
$$|\vec{v}(2)| = \sqrt{v_x^2 + v_y^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + 18^2} = \sqrt{64 + 324} = \sqrt{388} \approx 19.70\text{ m/s}$$

**Conclusion:** At $t = 2\text{ s}$, the drone travels with a scalar speed of $19.70\text{ m/s}$ along vector direction $\langle 8, 18 \rangle\text{ m/s}$.

---

## 9.6 Chapter Mastery Problem Set

Complete step-by-step written solutions are provided immediately following the exercises.

### Tier 1: Polynomial Derivatives of Motion
- **Exercise 9.1:** A particle's position is $s(t) = 4t^3 - 9t^2 + 6t - 15$. Find expressions for $v(t)$, $a(t)$, and $j(t)$.
- **Exercise 9.2:** For the particle in Exercise 9.1, find the velocity and acceleration at $t = 2$ seconds.

### Tier 2: Stationary Points and Turning Analysis
- **Exercise 9.3:** A drone moves along a line with position $s(t) = 2t^3 - 24t + 10$ meters ($t \ge 0$). Find when the drone stops and its position at that moment.
- **Exercise 9.4:** Determine whether the drone in Exercise 9.3 is speeding up or slowing down at $t = 1$ second.

### Tier 3: Rocket and Gravitational Flight
- **Exercise 9.5:** A flare is fired upward from a cliff: $h(t) = -4.9t^2 + 98t + 100$ meters. Find the maximum height reached and the time it takes to reach it.
- **Exercise 9.6:** For the flare in Exercise 9.5, calculate its impact velocity when it hits the water at ground level ($h = 0$).

### Tier 4: Higher-Order Derivative Diagnostics
- **Exercise 9.7:** Given $s(t) = \frac{1}{6}t^6 - \frac{1}{2}t^4$, compute the snap $s^{(4)}(t)$ at $t = 2$ seconds.
- **Exercise 9.8:** A robotic arm moves with angle $\theta(t) = 5\sin(4t)$ radians. Find the maximum angular jerk $|j_{\text{max}}|$.

### Tier 5: 2D Parametric Simulator Kinematics
- **Exercise 9.9:** A particle in a 2D physics engine follows $\vec{r}(t) = \langle 4\cos(2t), \ 4\sin(2t) \rangle$. Show that the acceleration vector $\vec{a}(t)$ is always pointing directly toward the origin and compute the constant scalar speed.
- **Exercise 9.10:** A high-speed train stops with position $s(t) = 60t - 2t^2 - \frac{1}{6}t^3$ for $t \ge 0$. Find the instant $t$ when the train comes to rest and evaluate whether jerk is non-zero at that moment.

---

## 9.7 Complete Step-by-Step Solutions to Chapter Exercises

### Solution to Exercise 9.1
For $s(t) = 4t^3 - 9t^2 + 6t - 15$:
1. $v(t) = s'(t) = 12t^2 - 18t + 6$.
2. $a(t) = v'(t) = 24t - 18$.
3. $j(t) = a'(t) = 24$.
**Final Answer:** $v(t) = 12t^2 - 18t + 6$, $a(t) = 24t - 18$, $j(t) = 24\text{ m/s}^3$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 9.2
Evaluate state at $t = 2$:
1. $v(2) = 12(2^2) - 18(2) + 6 = 12(4) - 36 + 6 = 48 - 36 + 6 = 18\text{ m/s}$.
2. $a(2) = 24(2) - 18 = 48 - 18 = 30\text{ m/s}^2$.
**Final Answer:** $v(2) = 18\text{ m/s}$, $a(2) = 30\text{ m/s}^2$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 9.3
For $s(t) = 2t^3 - 24t + 10$ ($t \ge 0$):
1. $v(t) = s'(t) = 6t^2 - 24$.
2. Set $v(t) = 0 \implies 6t^2 = 24 \implies t^2 = 4 \implies t = 2\text{ s}$ (since $t \ge 0$).
3. Position at $t = 2$:
$$s(2) = 2(2^3) - 24(2) + 10 = 2(8) - 48 + 10 = 16 - 48 + 10 = -22\text{ m}$$
**Final Answer:** Stops at $t = 2$ seconds at position $s = -22$ meters.

---

### Solution to Exercise 9.4
Test speeding up vs slowing down at $t = 1$:
1. $v(1) = 6(1^2) - 24 = 6 - 24 = -18\text{ m/s}$ ($v < 0$, moving backward).
2. $a(t) = 12t \implies a(1) = 12(1) = +12\text{ m/s}^2$ ($a > 0$).
3. $v(1) \cdot a(1) = (-18)(+12) = -216 < 0$.
Because signs are opposite, the drone is slowing down.
**Final Answer:** Slowing down.

---

### Solution to Exercise 9.5
For $h(t) = -4.9t^2 + 98t + 100$:
1. $v(t) = h'(t) = -9.8t + 98$.
2. Maximum height occurs when $v(t) = 0$:
$$-9.8t + 98 = 0 \implies 9.8t = 98 \implies t = 10\text{ seconds}$$
3. Maximum height:
$$h(10) = -4.9(10^2) + 98(10) + 100 = -490 + 980 + 100 = 590\text{ meters}$$
**Final Answer:** Maximum height is $590$ meters at $t = 10$ seconds.

---

### Solution to Exercise 9.6
Find impact velocity at $h = 0$:
1. Solve $-4.9t^2 + 98t + 100 = 0 \implies 4.9t^2 - 98t - 100 = 0$.
Using quadratic formula:
$$t = \frac{98 \pm \sqrt{(-98)^2 - 4(4.9)(-100)}}{2(4.9)} = \frac{98 \pm \sqrt{9604 + 1960}}{9.8} = \frac{98 \pm \sqrt{11564}}{9.8}$$
$$\sqrt{11564} \approx 107.536 \implies t = \frac{98 + 107.536}{9.8} = \frac{205.536}{9.8} \approx 20.973\text{ s}$$
2. Evaluate velocity at $t \approx 20.973$:
$$v(20.973) = -9.8(20.973) + 98 \approx -205.536 + 98 = -107.54\text{ m/s}$$
**Final Answer:** Impact velocity is $\approx -107.54\text{ m/s}$ (speed of $\approx 107.54\text{ m/s}$ downward).

---

### Solution to Exercise 9.7
For $s(t) = \frac{1}{6}t^6 - \frac{1}{2}t^4$:
- $s'(t) = t^5 - 2t^3$
- $s''(t) = 5t^4 - 6t^2$
- $s'''(t) = 20t^3 - 12t$
- $s^{(4)}(t) = 60t^2 - 12$
Evaluate at $t = 2$:
$$s^{(4)}(2) = 60(2^2) - 12 = 60(4) - 12 = 240 - 12 = 228\text{ m/s}^4$$
**Final Answer:** $228\text{ m/s}^4$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 9.8
For $\theta(t) = 5\sin(4t)$:
1. $\omega(t) = \theta'(t) = 5(4\cos(4t)) = 20\cos(4t)$.
2. $\alpha(t) = \theta''(t) = 20(-4\sin(4t)) = -80\sin(4t)$.
3. $j(t) = \theta'''(t) = -80(4\cos(4t)) = -320\cos(4t)$.
4. The maximum magnitude of cosine is $1$, so $|j_{\text{max}}| = 320\text{ rad/s}^3$.
**Final Answer:** $320\text{ rad/s}^3$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 9.9
For $\vec{r}(t) = \langle 4\cos(2t), 4\sin(2t) \rangle$:
1. Velocity:
$$\vec{v}(t) = \langle -8\sin(2t), 8\cos(2t) \rangle$$
2. Scalar speed:
$$|\vec{v}(t)| = \sqrt{(-8\sin(2t))^2 + (8\cos(2t))^2} = \sqrt{64\sin^2(2t) + 64\cos^2(2t)} = \sqrt{64(1)} = 8\text{ m/s} \quad (\text{Constant!})$$
3. Acceleration:
$$\vec{a}(t) = \langle -16\cos(2t), -16\sin(2t) \rangle = -4 \cdot \langle 4\cos(2t), 4\sin(2t) \rangle = -4 \vec{r}(t)$$
Because $\vec{a}(t) = -4\vec{r}(t)$, the acceleration is directed strictly toward the origin with magnitude $|\vec{a}| = 16\text{ m/s}^2 = \frac{v^2}{r} = \frac{8^2}{4} = \frac{64}{4} = 16$. $\blacksquare$
**Final Answer:** Acceleration is centripetal ($\vec{a}(t) = -4\vec{r}(t)$), and speed is constant at $8\text{ m/s}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 9.10
For $s(t) = 60t - 2t^2 - \frac{1}{6}t^3$:
1. $v(t) = 60 - 4t - \frac{1}{2}t^2$.
2. Set $v(t) = 0 \implies \frac{1}{2}t^2 + 4t - 60 = 0 \implies t^2 + 8t - 120 = 0$.
3. Factor: $(t + 20)(t - 6) = 0 \implies t = 6\text{ seconds}$ (since $t \ge 0$).
4. Compute jerk $j(t)$:
- $a(t) = v'(t) = -4 - t$
- $j(t) = a'(t) = -1\text{ m/s}^3$
At $t = 6\text{ s}$, $j(6) = -1\text{ m/s}^3 \neq 0$.
**Final Answer:** Train stops at $t = 6$ seconds; jerk is non-zero ($j = -1\text{ m/s}^3$).


---\n
# Chapter 10: Extrema, Concavity, and the Geometry of Optimization

> *"To find the peak of a mountain without climbing every inch of the range is the crowning achievement of differential calculus. Where the slope flattens to absolute zero, the world pauses between ascent and descent â€” offering the engineer the exact design point where efficiency is maximized, material is conserved, and performance peaks."*

---

## 10.1 Fermat's Theorem and the Hunt for Extremes

In engineering design and physics simulation, the central question is often: *What is the best possible configuration?*
- How can we shape an aerodynamic wing to maximize lift while minimizing drag?
- What dimensions for a pressure vessel minimize manufacturing cost while holding a given volume?
- At what throttle setting does an engine achieve maximum thermal efficiency?

Mathematically, these questions ask for **extrema**: local or absolute maxima and minima of a function.

In 1638, the French mathematician Pierre de Fermat discovered the foundational principle:

> **Fermat's Theorem on Stationary Points:**
> If a function $f(x)$ attains a local maximum or local minimum at an interior point $x = c$, and if the derivative $f'(c)$ exists, then:
> $$f'(c) = 0$$

### Why Must the Derivative Be Zero at a Peak?
Consider a smooth curve $y = f(x)$. If $f(c)$ is a local maximum, then for all points immediately to the left ($x < c$), $f(x) \le f(c)$, meaning the secant slope $\frac{f(x) - f(c)}{x - c} \ge 0$. As $x \to c^-$, this forces the left-hand derivative to be non-negative:

$$f'_-(c) \ge 0$$

Similarly, for all points immediately to the right ($x > c$), $f(x) \le f(c)$, meaning the secant slope $\frac{f(x) - f(c)}{x - c} \le 0$. As $x \to c^+$, this forces the right-hand derivative to be non-positive:

$$f'_+(c) \le 0$$

If $f$ is differentiable at $c$, both one-sided derivatives must agree:

$$0 \le f'(c) \le 0 \implies f'(c) = 0$$

At the very crest of a hill or the lowest trough of a valley, the tangent line must be perfectly horizontal.

```
       y ^            Local Max (f'(c) = 0)
         |                 _.-* -._
         |               .'        '.
         |              /            \             Local Min (f'(c) = 0)
         |             /              \                 _.-* -._
         |            /                '._           _.'        '.
         |                                '-._-*-..-'             \
         +-------------------------------------------------------------> x
                                              Critical Points
```

---

## 10.2 Critical Points: The Set of All Candidates

Fermat's theorem tells us where extrema *can* occur, but it does not guarantee that every horizontal tangent is an extremum.

A **critical point** of a function $f$ is a number $c$ in the domain of $f$ such that either:
1. $f'(c) = 0$ (a **stationary point**), or
2. $f'(c)$ does not exist (a **singular point**, such as a sharp corner or vertical cusp).

### The Saddle Warning: Not All Critical Points Are Extrema
Consider $f(x) = x^3$. Its derivative is $f'(x) = 3x^2$.
Setting $f'(x) = 0$ yields $x = 0$.
However, $f(0) = 0$, and for $x > 0$, $x^3 > 0$, while for $x < 0$, $x^3 < 0$. The point $(0, 0)$ is neither a local maximum nor a local minimum! It is a **horizontal inflection point** (a saddle terrace). The curve pauses momentarily flat, then continues climbing.

Therefore, finding critical points is only step one; we must subject each candidate to a definitive classification test.

---

## 10.3 The Extreme Value Theorem and the Closed Interval Method

Before classifying local hills and valleys, we address the global question: *Is an absolute maximum or minimum guaranteed to exist?*

> **The Extreme Value Theorem (EVT):**
> If a real-valued function $f$ is continuous on a closed, bounded interval $[a, b]$, then $f$ attains both an **absolute maximum** and an **absolute minimum** at least once on $[a, b]$.

### The Closed Interval Method
To find the absolute maximum and minimum of a continuous function $f$ on $[a, b]$:
1. Find all critical points of $f$ that lie inside the open interval $(a, b)$.
2. Evaluate $f(c)$ at each interior critical point.
3. Evaluate $f(a)$ and $f(b)$ at the two endpoints.
4. The largest of these calculated values is the **absolute maximum**; the smallest is the **absolute minimum**.

This method requires zero second-derivative calculations; it is a direct numerical comparison.

---

## 10.4 The First Derivative Test for Local Extrema

To classify an isolated critical point $c$ on an open interval:

> **The First Derivative Test:**
> Suppose $f$ is continuous at a critical point $c$ and differentiable on an open interval containing $c$ (except possibly at $c$ itself):
> 1. If $f'(x)$ changes from **positive to negative** as $x$ increases through $c$, then $f(c)$ is a **Local Maximum**.
> 2. If $f'(x)$ changes from **negative to positive** as $x$ increases through $c$, then $f(c)$ is a **Local Minimum**.
> 3. If $f'(x)$ **does not change sign** (remains positive on both sides or negative on both sides), then $f(c)$ is **neither an extremum**.

*Physical intuition:* If you are walking up a slope ($f' > 0$) and then start walking down ($f' < 0$), you must have crossed a mountain peak. If you walk down into a gully ($f' < 0$) and then begin climbing ($f' > 0$), you were at the bottom of the valley.

---

## 10.5 Concavity and the Second Derivative Test

While the first derivative tracks the slope of the curve, the **second derivative** tracks how that slope is bending.

### Concavity Defined
- A curve is **concave upward** (opening upward like a cup, $\cup$) on an interval if its tangent lines lie *below* the curve. This occurs wherever the slope $f'(x)$ is strictly increasing:
  $$f''(x) > 0 \iff \text{Concave Upward}$$
- A curve is **concave downward** (opening downward like an umbrella, $\cap$) on an interval if its tangent lines lie *above* the curve. This occurs wherever the slope $f'(x)$ is strictly decreasing:
  $$f''(x) < 0 \iff \text{Concave Downward}$$

### Inflection Points
An **inflection point** is a point on a curve where the concavity changes from upward to downward, or from downward to upward. At an inflection point:
- The second derivative typically equals zero: $f''(c) = 0$ (or $f''(c)$ fails to exist).
- The tangent line actually cuts straight through the curve!

```
       Concave Down (f'' < 0)               Concave Up (f'' > 0)
            _.-""-._                                   \       /
          .'        '.             Inflection Point     \     /
         /            \                * (f'' = 0)       '._.'
        /              \             .'
       +----------------------------+--------------------------> x
```

### The Second Derivative Test for Local Extrema
If $f'(c) = 0$ and $f''(c)$ is continuous near $c$:
1. If $f''(c) < 0$, the curve is concave downward at the horizontal tangent $\implies$ $f(c)$ is a **Local Maximum**.
2. If $f''(c) > 0$, the curve is concave upward at the horizontal tangent $\implies$ $f(c)$ is a **Local Minimum**.
3. If $f''(c) = 0$, the test is **inconclusive** (the point could be a maximum like $-x^4$, a minimum like $+x^4$, or an inflection point like $x^3$). You must revert to the First Derivative Test.

---

## 10.6 The Seven Steps of Applied Optimization

Real-world engineering problems do not arrive with equations ready to differentiate. You must construct the model yourself:

1. **Understand the Objective:** Identify what quantity must be maximized (e.g., volume, efficiency, range) or minimized (e.g., cost, surface area, power loss).
2. **Assign Variables & Draw a Diagram:** Label all dimensions, rates, and geometric constraints.
3. **Write the Primary Objective Function:** Express the target quantity in terms of the variables: $Q = f(x, y)$.
4. **Identify Constraint Equations:** Use geometry or physical laws to relate the variables: $g(x, y) = C$.
5. **Eliminate Extra Variables:** Substitute the constraint into the objective equation to express the target in terms of **a single independent variable**: $Q = f(x)$.
6. **Establish the Feasible Physical Domain:** Determine the allowable values for $x$ (e.g., lengths cannot be negative, angles are bounded: $x \in [0, L]$).
7. **Differentiate, Find Critical Points, and Test:** Compute $f'(x) = 0$, evaluate boundaries, and verify that the critical point is indeed the global optimum.

---

## 10.7 Ten Progressive Worked Numerical Examples

### Example 10.1: Absolute Extrema on a Closed Interval
**Problem:** Find the absolute maximum and absolute minimum of $f(x) = 2x^3 - 3x^2 - 12x + 5$ on the closed interval $[-2, 4]$.

**Step 1:** Compute the derivative $f'(x)$:
$$f'(x) = 6x^2 - 6x - 12 = 6(x^2 - x - 2) = 6(x - 2)(x + 1)$$

**Step 2:** Find critical points by setting $f'(x) = 0$:
$$6(x - 2)(x + 1) = 0 \implies x_1 = 2, \quad x_2 = -1$$
Both critical points lie inside the open interval $(-2, 4)$.

**Step 3:** Evaluate $f(x)$ at all critical points and interval endpoints:
1. **Left Endpoint ($x = -2$):**
   $$f(-2) = 2(-2)^3 - 3(-2)^2 - 12(-2) + 5 = 2(-8) - 3(4) + 24 + 5 = -16 - 12 + 24 + 5 = 1$$
2. **Critical Point ($x = -1$):**
   $$f(-1) = 2(-1)^3 - 3(-1)^2 - 12(-1) + 5 = -2 - 3 + 12 + 5 = 12$$
3. **Critical Point ($x = 2$):**
   $$f(2) = 2(2^3) - 3(2^2) - 12(2) + 5 = 16 - 12 - 24 + 5 = -15$$
4. **Right Endpoint ($x = 4$):**
   $$f(4) = 2(4^3) - 3(4^2) - 12(4) + 5 = 2(64) - 3(16) - 48 + 5 = 128 - 48 - 48 + 5 = 37$$

**Step 4:** Compare the values:
- Maximum value among $\{1, 12, -15, 37\}$ is **$37$** (at $x = 4$).
- Minimum value among $\{1, 12, -15, 37\}$ is **$-15$** (at $x = 2$).

**Conclusion:** Absolute maximum is $37$ at $x = 4$; absolute minimum is $-15$ at $x = 2$.

---

### Example 10.2: First Derivative Test on a Quartic Curve
**Problem:** Classify all local extrema of $f(x) = x^4 - 4x^3 + 10$.

**Step 1:** Compute $f'(x)$:
$$f'(x) = 4x^3 - 12x^2 = 4x^2(x - 3)$$

**Step 2:** Find critical points ($f'(x) = 0$):
$$4x^2(x - 3) = 0 \implies x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad x = 3$$

**Step 3:** Set up sign intervals for $f'(x) = 4x^2(x - 3)$:
Notice that $4x^2 \ge 0$ for all $x$. The sign of $f'(x)$ is dictated entirely by $(x - 3)$:
- For $x < 0$: $4x^2 > 0$ and $(x - 3) < 0 \implies f'(x) < 0$ (Decreasing).
- For $0 < x < 3$: $4x^2 > 0$ and $(x - 3) < 0 \implies f'(x) < 0$ (Decreasing).
- For $x > 3$: $4x^2 > 0$ and $(x - 3) > 0 \implies f'(x) > 0$ (Increasing).

**Step 4:** Classify each critical point:
- At $x = 0$: $f'(x)$ is negative on the left and negative on the right. The slope does not change sign. Therefore, $x = 0$ is **neither a local maximum nor a local minimum** (it is a horizontal inflection point).
- At $x = 3$: $f'(x)$ changes from negative to positive. By the First Derivative Test, $x = 3$ is a **Local Minimum**.

**Step 5:** Compute value at the local minimum:
$$f(3) = 3^4 - 4(3^3) + 10 = 81 - 108 + 10 = -17$$

**Conclusion:** Local minimum of $-17$ at $(3, -17)$; horizontal inflection at $(0, 10)$.

---

### Example 10.3: Second Derivative Test for Multiple Extrema
**Problem:** Use the Second Derivative Test to classify the local extrema of $f(x) = 3x^5 - 5x^3 + 2$.

**Step 1:** First derivative:
$$f'(x) = 15x^4 - 15x^2 = 15x^2(x^2 - 1) = 15x^2(x - 1)(x + 1)$$
Critical points are $x = -1, 0, 1$.

**Step 2:** Second derivative:
$$f''(x) = 60x^3 - 30x = 30x(2x^2 - 1)$$

**Step 3:** Evaluate $f''(x)$ at each critical point:
- **At $x = -1$:**
  $$f''(-1) = 30(-1)(2(-1)^2 - 1) = -30(2 - 1) = -30 < 0$$
  Since $f''(-1) < 0$, the curve is concave downward. Thus, $x = -1$ is a **Local Maximum**.
  $$f(-1) = 3(-1)^5 - 5(-1)^3 + 2 = -3 + 5 + 2 = 4$$

- **At $x = 1$:**
  $$f''(1) = 30(1)(2(1)^2 - 1) = 30(1) = +30 > 0$$
  Since $f''(1) > 0$, the curve is concave upward. Thus, $x = 1$ is a **Local Minimum**.
  $$f(1) = 3(1)^5 - 5(1)^3 + 2 = 3 - 5 + 2 = 0$$

- **At $x = 0$:**
  $$f''(0) = 30(0)(0 - 1) = 0$$
  The Second Derivative Test is **inconclusive**. Revert to First Derivative Test:
  For $x \in (-1, 0)$, $f'(x) = 15x^2(x^2 - 1) = (+)(-) = -$.
  For $x \in (0, 1)$, $f'(x) = 15x^2(x^2 - 1) = (+)(-) = -$.
  Since $f'$ remains negative on both sides of $x = 0$, it is **neither** an extremum.

**Conclusion:** Local maximum at $(-1, 4)$; local minimum at $(1, 0)$.

---

### Example 10.4: Locating Inflection Points and Concavity Intervals
**Problem:** Find all intervals of concavity and inflection points for $f(x) = x^4 - 6x^2 + 8x - 3$.

**Step 1:** Compute derivatives:
$$f'(x) = 4x^3 - 12x + 8$$
$$f''(x) = 12x^2 - 12 = 12(x^2 - 1) = 12(x - 1)(x + 1)$$

**Step 2:** Set $f''(x) = 0$:
$$12(x - 1)(x + 1) = 0 \implies x = -1 \quad \text{and} \quad x = 1$$

**Step 3:** Determine concavity intervals:
- On $(-\infty, -1)$: Test $x = -2 \implies f''(-2) = 12((-2)^2 - 1) = 12(3) = 36 > 0 \implies$ **Concave Upward**.
- On $(-1, 1)$: Test $x = 0 \implies f''(0) = 12(0 - 1) = -12 < 0 \implies$ **Concave Downward**.
- On $(1, \infty)$: Test $x = 2 \implies f''(2) = 12(4 - 1) = 36 > 0 \implies$ **Concave Upward**.

**Step 4:** Compute inflection point coordinates:
Because $f''(x)$ changes sign across both $x = -1$ and $x = 1$, both are genuine inflection points:
- $f(-1) = (-1)^4 - 6(-1)^2 + 8(-1) - 3 = 1 - 6 - 8 - 3 = -16 \implies (-1, -16)$
- $f(1) = 1^4 - 6(1)^2 + 8(1) - 3 = 1 - 6 + 8 - 3 = 0 \implies (1, 0)$

---

### Example 10.5: Engineering Box Optimization (Titanium Sheet Folding)
**Problem:** A lightweight drone payload enclosure is manufactured from a rectangular titanium sheet measuring $24\text{ cm} \times 36\text{ cm}$. Equal squares of side length $x$ are cut from all four corners, and the remaining flaps are folded up to form an open-top rectangular container. Find the cut size $x$ that maximizes the container volume, and calculate that maximum volume.

**Step 1:** Geometric setup:
- Length of base: $L(x) = 36 - 2x$
- Width of base: $W(x) = 24 - 2x$
- Height of box: $H(x) = x$

**Step 2:** Volume function:
$$V(x) = x(36 - 2x)(24 - 2x) = x(864 - 72x - 48x + 4x^2) = x(864 - 120x + 4x^2)$$
$$V(x) = 4x^3 - 120x^2 + 864x$$

**Step 3:** Feasible physical domain:
All dimensions must be positive: $x > 0$, $24 - 2x > 0 \implies x < 12$.
Domain: $x \in [0, 12]$. At endpoints $x = 0$ and $x = 12$, volume is $V = 0$.

**Step 4:** Differentiate to find critical points:
$$V'(x) = 12x^2 - 240x + 864$$
Set $V'(x) = 0$ and divide through by $12$:
$$x^2 - 20x + 72 = 0$$

**Step 5:** Solve using the quadratic formula:
$$x = \frac{20 \pm \sqrt{(-20)^2 - 4(1)(72)}}{2} = \frac{20 \pm \sqrt{400 - 288}}{2} = \frac{20 \pm \sqrt{112}}{2}$$
Notice that $\sqrt{112} = \sqrt{16 \times 7} = 4\sqrt{7} \approx 10.583$:
$$x = 10 \pm 2\sqrt{7}$$
- $x_1 = 10 + 2\sqrt{7} \approx 15.29\text{ cm}$ (Outside the physical domain $[0, 12]$)
- $x_2 = 10 - 2\sqrt{7} \approx 10 - 5.2915 = 4.7085\text{ cm}$ (Feasible!)

**Step 6:** Verify maximum via second derivative:
$$V''(x) = 24x - 240$$
$$V''(4.7085) = 24(4.7085) - 240 = 113.0 - 240 = -127.0 < 0 \implies \text{Confirmed Maximum}$$

**Step 7:** Calculate optimal dimensions and maximum volume:
- Height: $x \approx 4.71\text{ cm}$
- Length: $36 - 2(4.7085) = 36 - 9.417 = 26.583\text{ cm}$
- Width: $24 - 2(4.7085) = 24 - 9.417 = 14.583\text{ cm}$
- Maximum Volume:
  $$V_{\text{max}} = (4.7085) \times (26.583) \times (14.583) \approx 1825.29\text{ cm}^3$$

---

### Example 10.6: Material Optimization of a Cylindrical Propellant Tank
**Problem:** A closed cylindrical rocket propellant tank must hold a fixed volume of $V = 16\pi\text{ m}^3$. Find the radius $r$ and height $h$ that minimize the total surface area $A$ (and thus minimize hull mass).

**Step 1:** Objective function (Surface Area of closed cylinder):
$$A = 2\pi r^2 + 2\pi r h$$

**Step 2:** Constraint equation (Volume):
$$V = \pi r^2 h = 16\pi \implies r^2 h = 16 \implies h = \frac{16}{r^2}$$

**Step 3:** Substitute constraint into area function:
$$A(r) = 2\pi r^2 + 2\pi r \left(\frac{16}{r^2}\right) = 2\pi r^2 + \frac{32\pi}{r} = 2\pi r^2 + 32\pi r^{-1}$$
Domain: $r \in (0, \infty)$.

**Step 4:** Differentiate with respect to $r$:
$$A'(r) = 4\pi r - 32\pi r^{-2} = 4\pi r - \frac{32\pi}{r^2}$$

**Step 5:** Set $A'(r) = 0$:
$$4\pi r = \frac{32\pi}{r^2} \implies 4\pi r^3 = 32\pi \implies r^3 = 8 \implies r = 2\text{ meters}$$

**Step 6:** Compute optimal height:
$$h = \frac{16}{r^2} = \frac{16}{2^2} = \frac{16}{4} = 4\text{ meters}$$

Notice that $h = 2r$ (height equals diameter). This is a universal geometric invariant for closed cylinders minimizing surface area!

**Step 7:** Second derivative verification:
$$A''(r) = 4\pi + \frac{64\pi}{r^3} \implies A''(2) = 4\pi + \frac{64\pi}{8} = 4\pi + 8\pi = 12\pi > 0 \implies \text{Global Minimum}$$

---

### Example 10.7: Maximum Power Transfer in Electrical Transmission
**Problem:** An electrical generator with electromotive force $E$ and internal resistance $r$ delivers electrical power to an external load resistor $R$ according to:
$$P(R) = \frac{E^2 R}{(R + r)^2}$$
Find the load resistance $R$ that maximizes power delivered to the load.

**Step 1:** Regard $E$ and $r$ as positive constants. Differentiate $P(R)$ with respect to $R$ using the quotient rule:
$$u = E^2 R \implies u' = E^2$$
$$v = (R + r)^2 \implies v' = 2(R + r)$$
$$\frac{dP}{dR} = \frac{(R + r)^2(E^2) - (E^2 R)[2(R + r)]}{(R + r)^4}$$

**Step 2:** Factor out $(R + r)$ from the numerator:
$$\frac{dP}{dR} = \frac{(R + r) \cdot [E^2(R + r) - 2E^2 R]}{(R + r)^4} = \frac{E^2(R + r - 2R)}{(R + r)^3} = \frac{E^2(r - R)}{(R + r)^3}$$

**Step 3:** Set $\frac{dP}{dR} = 0$:
$$E^2(r - R) = 0 \implies R = r$$

**Step 4:** First derivative sign test:
- For $R < r$: $r - R > 0 \implies \frac{dP}{dR} > 0$ (Power increasing).
- For $R > r$: $r - R < 0 \implies \frac{dP}{dR} < 0$ (Power decreasing).
By the First Derivative Test, $R = r$ is the absolute global maximum.

**Conclusion:** Maximum power transfer occurs when the load resistance equals the internal resistance ($R = r$). This is the famous **Jacobi Maximum Power Transfer Theorem** in electrical engineering.

---

### Example 10.8: Minimizing Autonomous Cargo Ship Transit Cost
**Problem:** The operating cost per kilometer of an autonomous electric cargo vessel is modeled by:
$$C(v) = \frac{1800}{v} + 0.5 v^2$$
where $v$ is cruising speed in knots ($v > 0$). The first term represents fixed crewless telemetry and hotel load amortized over speed; the second term represents hydrodynamic hull drag. Determine the most economical cruising speed.

**Step 1:** Rewrite $C(v) = 1800 v^{-1} + 0.5 v^2$.
**Step 2:** Differentiate with respect to $v$:
$$C'(v) = -1800 v^{-2} + 1.0 v = -\frac{1800}{v^2} + v$$

**Step 3:** Set $C'(v) = 0$:
$$v = \frac{1800}{v^2} \implies v^3 = 1800 \implies v = \sqrt[3]{1800} \approx 12.164\text{ knots}$$

**Step 4:** Confirm minimum via second derivative:
$$C''(v) = \frac{3600}{v^3} + 1.0$$
Since $v > 0$, $C''(v) > 0$ everywhere, guaranteeing a unique global minimum.

**Step 5:** Calculate minimum operating cost:
$$C(12.164) = \frac{1800}{12.164} + 0.5(12.164)^2 \approx 147.98 + 73.99 \approx \$221.97\text{ per km}$$

---

### Example 10.9: Extrema Involving Non-Differentiable Critical Points
**Problem:** Find all absolute extrema of $f(x) = x^{2/3}(5 - x)$ on the interval $[-1, 5]$.

**Step 1:** Expand $f(x)$:
$$f(x) = 5x^{2/3} - x^{5/3}$$

**Step 2:** Differentiate:
$$f'(x) = 5\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)x^{-1/3} - \frac{5}{3}x^{2/3} = \frac{10}{3x^{1/3}} - \frac{5x^{2/3}}{3}$$

**Step 3:** Place over a common denominator $3x^{1/3}$:
$$f'(x) = \frac{10 - 5x}{3x^{1/3}} = \frac{5(2 - x)}{3\sqrt[3]{x}}$$

**Step 4:** Find all critical points in $[-1, 5]$:
1. **Numerator $= 0$:** $2 - x = 0 \implies x = 2$.
2. **Denominator $= 0$ (Derivative DNE):** $3\sqrt[3]{x} = 0 \implies x = 0$.
Both $x = 0$ and $x = 2$ are valid critical points!

**Step 5:** Evaluate $f(x)$ at critical points and endpoints:
- Left endpoint ($x = -1$): $f(-1) = (-1)^{2/3}(5 - (-1)) = (1)(6) = 6$.
- Singular critical point ($x = 0$): $f(0) = 0^{2/3}(5 - 0) = 0$.
- Stationary critical point ($x = 2$): $f(2) = 2^{2/3}(5 - 2) = 3\sqrt[3]{4} \approx 3(1.5874) \approx 4.762$.
- Right endpoint ($x = 5$): $f(5) = 5^{2/3}(5 - 5) = 0$.

**Step 6:** Identify extrema:
- Absolute Maximum: **$6$** at $x = -1$.
- Absolute Minimum: **$0$** at both $x = 0$ and $x = 5$.

---

### Example 10.10: Fiber-Optic Cable Routing Optimization
**Problem:** Two scientific research stations $A$ and $B$ are located on opposite banks of a straight river of width $W = 3\text{ km}$. Station $B$ is located $L = 8\text{ km}$ downstream from station $A$. Laying underwater cable costs $\$50,000$ per km, while laying land cable costs $\$30,000$ per km. The cable runs underwater from $A$ to a point $P$ on the opposite bank distance $x$ downstream from $A$, and then along the bank from $P$ to $B$. Find the distance $x$ that minimizes the total cost.

```
       Station A
          *
          | \
      3 km|  \  Underwater ($50k/km)
          |   \
          +----+------------------------* Station B
          0    P                        8 km
               <-- x --> <--- 8 - x --->
```

**Step 1:** Formulate the cost function:
- Underwater distance: $D_{\text{water}} = \sqrt{3^2 + x^2} = \sqrt{x^2 + 9}$
- Overland distance: $D_{\text{land}} = 8 - x$
- Total cost in thousands of dollars:
  $$C(x) = 50\sqrt{x^2 + 9} + 30(8 - x) \quad \text{for } x \in [0, 8]$$

**Step 2:** Differentiate with respect to $x$:
$$C'(x) = 50 \cdot \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x^2 + 9}} \cdot (2x) - 30 = \frac{50x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 9}} - 30$$

**Step 3:** Set $C'(x) = 0$:
$$\frac{50x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 9}} = 30 \implies \frac{5x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 9}} = 3$$
Cross-multiply and square both sides:
$$5x = 3\sqrt{x^2 + 9} \implies 25x^2 = 9(x^2 + 9) = 9x^2 + 81$$
$$16x^2 = 81 \implies x^2 = \frac{81}{16} \implies x = \frac{9}{4} = 2.25\text{ km}$$

**Step 4:** Compare with endpoints:
- At $x = 2.25\text{ km}$:
  $$C(2.25) = 50\sqrt{2.25^2 + 9} + 30(8 - 2.25) = 50(3.75) + 30(5.75) = 187.5 + 172.5 = \$360,000$$
- At $x = 0$ (all overland after direct crossing):
  $$C(0) = 50(3) + 30(8) = 150 + 240 = \$390,000$$
- At $x = 8$ (all underwater straight to $B$):
  $$C(8) = 50\sqrt{8^2 + 3^2} + 0 = 50\sqrt{73} \approx 50(8.544) = \$427,200$$

**Conclusion:** The minimum cost is $\$360,000$, achieved by aiming the underwater cable to land $2.25\text{ km}$ downstream.

---

- **Exercise 10.2:** Find all critical points of $g(x) = x^4 - 8x^2 + 5$.

### Tier 2: Classification via First and Second Derivative Tests
- **Exercise 10.3:** Classify all local extrema of $f(x) = x^3 - 3x^2 - 9x + 2$ using the Second Derivative Test.

- **Exercise 10.8:** A printed poster must have a total area of $1800\text{ cm}^2$ with top and bottom margins of $5\text{ cm}$ and side margins of $4\text{ cm}$. Find the poster dimensions that maximize the printable text area.

- **Exercise 10.9:** The illumination $I$ of a point source on a horizontal desk at distance $x$ from the base of a lamp pole of height $h$ is $I(h) = \frac{k h}{(h^2 + x^2)^{3/2}}$. For a fixed horizontal distance $x = 4\text{ m}$, find the pole height $h$ that maximizes illumination.
- **Exercise 10.10:** Find all points on the parabola $y = x^2$ that are closest to the coordinate point $(0, 2)$.

## 10.9 Complete Step-by-Step Solutions to Chapter Exercises

### Solution to Exercise 10.1
Find critical points of $f(x) = 2x^3 - 9x^2 + 12x - 7$.
1. Compute $f'(x) = 6x^2 - 18x + 12$.
2. Set $f'(x) = 0 \implies 6(x^2 - 3x + 2) = 0 \implies 6(x - 1)(x - 2) = 0$.
**Final Answer:** Critical points are $x = 1$ and $x = 2$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 10.2
Find critical points of $g(x) = x^4 - 8x^2 + 5$.
1. Compute $g'(x) = 4x^3 - 16x$.
2. Set $g'(x) = 0 \implies 4x(x^2 - 4) = 0 \implies 4x(x - 2)(x + 2) = 0$.
**Final Answer:** Critical points are $x = -2, 0, 2$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 10.3
Classify extrema of $f(x) = x^3 - 3x^2 - 9x + 2$.
1. $f'(x) = 3x^2 - 6x - 9 = 3(x^2 - 2x - 3) = 3(x - 3)(x + 1) = 0 \implies x = 3, -1$.
2. $f''(x) = 6x - 6$.
- At $x = -1$: $f''(-1) = 6(-1) - 6 = -12 < 0 \implies$ **Local Maximum**.
  $$f(-1) = (-1)^3 - 3(-1)^2 - 9(-1) + 2 = -1 - 3 + 9 + 2 = 7$$
- At $x = 3$: $f''(3) = 6(3) - 6 = 12 > 0 \implies$ **Local Minimum**.
  $$f(3) = 3^3 - 3(3^2) - 9(3) + 2 = 27 - 27 - 27 + 2 = -25$$
**Final Answer:** Local Maximum at $(-1, 7)$; Local Minimum at $(3, -25)$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 10.4
Find concavity and inflection points for $y = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x$.
1. $y' = 3x^2 - 12x + 9$.
2. $y'' = 6x - 12 = 6(x - 2)$.
3. Setting $y'' = 0 \implies x = 2$.
- For $x < 2$: $y'' < 0 \implies$ **Concave Downward** on $(-\infty, 2)$.
- For $x > 2$: $y'' > 0 \implies$ **Concave Upward** on $(2, \infty)$.
4. Inflection point: $y(2) = 2^3 - 6(2^2) + 9(2) = 8 - 24 + 18 = 2 \implies (2, 2)$.
**Final Answer:** Concave down on $(-\infty, 2)$, concave up on $(2, \infty)$, inflection point at $(2, 2)$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 10.5
Find absolute extrema of $f(x) = x^3 - 12x$ on $[-3, 5]$.
1. $f'(x) = 3x^2 - 12 = 3(x^2 - 4) = 0 \implies x = \pm 2$. Both are inside $(-3, 5)$.
2. Evaluate:
- $f(-3) = (-3)^3 - 12(-3) = -27 + 36 = 9$.
- $f(-2) = (-2)^3 - 12(-2) = -8 + 24 = 16$.
- $f(2) = 2^3 - 12(2) = 8 - 24 = -16$.
- $f(5) = 5^3 - 12(5) = 125 - 60 = 65$.
3. Absolute max is $65$ (at $x = 5$). Absolute min is $-16$ (at $x = 2$).
**Final Answer:** Absolute maximum $= 65$; absolute minimum $= -16$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 10.6
Find absolute extrema of $f(x) = \frac{x}{x^2 + 1}$ on $[0, 3]$.
1. $f'(x) = \frac{(x^2+1)(1) - x(2x)}{(x^2+1)^2} = \frac{1 - x^2}{(x^2+1)^2} = 0 \implies x = 1$ (since $x \ge 0$).
2. Evaluate:
- $f(0) = \frac{0}{1} = 0$.
- $f(1) = \frac{1}{1^2 + 1} = \frac{1}{2} = 0.5$.
- $f(3) = \frac{3}{3^2 + 1} = \frac{3}{10} = 0.3$.
**Final Answer:** Absolute maximum $= 0.5$ (at $x = 1$); absolute minimum $= 0$ (at $x = 0$).

---

### Solution to Exercise 10.7
Maximize pasture area along river with $1200\text{ m}$ fencing.
1. River along length $L$; two widths $W$: $2W + L = 1200 \implies L = 1200 - 2W$.
2. Area: $A(W) = W(1200 - 2W) = 1200W - 2W^2$.
3. $A'(W) = 1200 - 4W = 0 \implies 4W = 1200 \implies W = 300\text{ m}$.
4. $L = 1200 - 2(300) = 600\text{ m}$.
5. Area: $300 \times 600 = 180,000\text{ m}^2$.
**Final Answer:** Width $W = 300\text{ m}$, Length $L = 600\text{ m}$, Maximum Area $= 180,000\text{ m}^2$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 10.8
Maximize printable area of poster with total area $1800\text{ cm}^2$ and margins (top/bottom $5$, sides $4$).
1. Let total width be $w$, total height be $h$: $w \cdot h = 1800 \implies h = \frac{1800}{w}$.
2. Printable dimensions: width $(w - 8)$, height $(h - 10)$.
3. Printable Area: $A(w) = (w - 8)\left(\frac{1800}{w} - 10\right) = 1800 - 10w - \frac{14400}{w} + 80 = 1880 - 10w - 14400w^{-1}$.
4. $A'(w) = -10 + 14400w^{-2} = 0 \implies 10 = \frac{14400}{w^2} \implies w^2 = 1440 \implies w = \sqrt{1440} = 12\sqrt{10} \approx 37.95\text{ cm}$.
5. Height: $h = \frac{1800}{12\sqrt{10}} = \frac{150}{\sqrt{10}} = 15\sqrt{10} \approx 47.43\text{ cm}$.
**Final Answer:** Total width $w = 12\sqrt{10} \approx 37.95\text{ cm}$, Total height $h = 15\sqrt{10} \approx 47.43\text{ cm}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 10.9
Maximize illumination $I(h) = \frac{k h}{(h^2 + 16)^{3/2}}$.
1. Let $u = h \implies u' = 1$, and $v = (h^2 + 16)^{3/2} \implies v' = \frac{3}{2}(h^2+16)^{1/2}(2h) = 3h\sqrt{h^2+16}$.
2. Quotient rule:
$$I'(h) = k \cdot \frac{(h^2+16)^{3/2}(1) - h[3h(h^2+16)^{1/2}]}{(h^2+16)^3}$$
Factor $(h^2+16)^{1/2}$:
$$I'(h) = k \cdot \frac{(h^2+16)^{1/2}[(h^2+16) - 3h^2]}{(h^2+16)^3} = k \cdot \frac{16 - 2h^2}{(h^2+16)^{5/2}}$$
3. Set $I'(h) = 0 \implies 16 - 2h^2 = 0 \implies 2h^2 = 16 \implies h^2 = 8 \implies h = \sqrt{8} = 2\sqrt{2} \approx 2.828\text{ m}$.
**Final Answer:** Pole height $h = 2\sqrt{2} \approx 2.83\text{ meters}$.

---

### Solution to Exercise 10.10
Find points on $y = x^2$ closest to $(0, 2)$.
1. Distance squared to $(0, 2)$:
$$D^2 = (x - 0)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = x^2 + (x^2 - 2)^2 = x^2 + x^4 - 4x^2 + 4 = x^4 - 3x^2 + 4$$
2. Minimize $f(x) = x^4 - 3x^2 + 4$:
$$f'(x) = 4x^3 - 6x = 2x(2x^2 - 3) = 0$$
Roots: $x = 0$ or $2x^2 = 3 \implies x^2 = \frac{3}{2} \implies x = \pm\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}} = \pm\frac{\sqrt{6}}{2}$.
3. Evaluate second derivative: $f''(x) = 12x^2 - 6$.
- At $x = 0$: $f''(0) = -6 < 0$ (Local maximum of distance squared!).
- At $x = \pm\sqrt{3/2}$: $f'' = 12(3/2) - 6 = 18 - 6 = 12 > 0$ (Local and global minimum!).
4. Corresponding $y$: $y = x^2 = \frac{3}{2} = 1.5$.
**Final Answer:** Points are $\left(\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}, \frac{3}{2}\right)$ and $\left(-\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}, \frac{3}{2}\right)$.


---\n
# Chapter 11: Numerical Differentiation, Taylor Series, and Floating-Point Error

> *"Analytic formulas exist in the pristine heaven of pure symbols, but reality runs on physical silicon. When a flight controller processes turbulent sensor streams, or a physics simulator integrates rigid-body collisions, there are no symbolic equations to differentiate â€” only discrete floating-point numbers subject to finite bit-depth, truncation, and roundoff noise."*

---

## 11.1 Beyond Analytic Symbols: Why Numerical Calculus is Essential

Throughout Chapters 4 through 10, we manipulated smooth symbolic formulas using algebraic rules. In practical science, robotics, and software engineering, you will constantly face situations where symbolic differentiation is impossible or impractical:

1. **Discrete Experimental Data:** An inertial measurement unit (IMU) on a drone outputs orientation samples at $500\text{ Hz}$. You must estimate angular velocity and acceleration from a stream of raw numbers.
2. **Black-Box Simulation Functions:** A finite-element aerodynamics engine computes drag given wing sweep angle $\alpha$, but has no closed-form algebraic formula.
3. **Loss Functions in Machine Learning:** Gradients must be estimated through complex computational graphs where numerical perturbation verifies backward-mode automatic differentiation.
4. **Non-Integrable / Transcendental Equations:** Finding equilibrium states where equations cannot be solved algebraically.

To compute derivatives in computer code, we must translate the continuous limit $\lim_{h \to 0}$ into discrete algorithms known as **finite difference stencils**.

---

## 11.2 Deriving Stencils from the Taylor Series

The **Taylor Series** is the master key to numerical analysis. Any sufficiently smooth function $f(x)$ can be expanded into an infinite polynomial series near $x$:

$$f(x + h) = f(x) + f'(x) h + \frac{f''(x)}{2!} h^2 + \frac{f'''(x)}{3!} h^3 + \frac{f^{(4)}(x)}{4!} h^4 + \dots$$

$$f(x - h) = f(x) - f'(x) h + \frac{f''(x)}{2!} h^2 - \frac{f'''(x)}{3!} h^3 + \frac{f^{(4)}(x)}{4!} h^4 - \dots$$

By taking linear combinations of these expansions, we systematically derive the three fundamental difference stencils.

### 1. The Forward Difference Stencil
From the expansion of $f(x+h)$:

$$f(x+h) - f(x) = f'(x) h + \frac{f''(x)}{2} h^2 + \mathcal{O}(h^3)$$

Divide both sides by $h$:

$$D_+ f(x) = \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} = f'(x) + \underbrace{\frac{f''(x)}{2} h}_{\text{Leading Error Term}} + \mathcal{O}(h^2)$$

Rearranging:

$$f'(x) = \frac{f(x+h) - f(x)}{h} + \mathcal{O}(h)$$

The error is proportional to the first power of step size $h$. We say the Forward Difference is **first-order accurate** ($\mathcal{O}(h)$). If you halve $h$, the error is cut in half ($2\times$ improvement).

### 2. The Backward Difference Stencil
From the expansion of $f(x-h)$:

$$f(x) - f(x-h) = f'(x) h - \frac{f''(x)}{2} h^2 + \mathcal{O}(h^3)$$

$$D_- f(x) = \frac{f(x) - f(x-h)}{h} = f'(x) - \frac{f''(x)}{2} h + \mathcal{O}(h^2)$$

The Backward Difference is also **first-order accurate** ($\mathcal{O}(h)$). In real-time physical simulation (where future data $f(x+h)$ has not yet arrived), backward difference is the only causal stencil available.

### 3. The Central Difference Stencil (The Sovereign Stencil)
Now subtract the $f(x-h)$ expansion from the $f(x+h)$ expansion:

$$f(x+h) - f(x-h) = \left[f(x) + f'(x) h + \frac{f''(x)}{2} h^2 + \frac{f'''(x)}{6} h^3 + \dots\right] - \left[f(x) - f'(x) h + \frac{f''(x)}{2} h^2 - \frac{f'''(x)}{6} h^3 + \dots\right]$$

Notice the symmetry:
- $f(x) - f(x) = 0$
- $f'(x) h - (-f'(x) h) = 2 f'(x) h$
- $\frac{f''(x)}{2} h^2 - \frac{f''(x)}{2} h^2 = 0 \quad \mathbf{(THE\ EVEN\ ERROR\ TERM\ CANCELS\ EXACTLY!)}$
- $\frac{f'''(x)}{6} h^3 - \left(-\frac{f'''(x)}{6} h^3\right) = \frac{2 f'''(x)}{6} h^3 = \frac{f'''(x)}{3} h^3$

Dividing by $2h$:

$$D_0 f(x) = \frac{f(x+h) - f(x-h)}{2h} = f'(x) + \frac{f'''(x)}{6} h^2 + \mathcal{O}(h^4)$$

$$f'(x) = \frac{f(x+h) - f(x-h)}{2h} + \mathcal{O}(h^2)$$

Because the $h^1$ error term vanished by geometric symmetry, the leading error term scales with **$h^2$**!
The Central Difference is **second-order accurate** ($\mathcal{O}(h^2)$).
If you halve $h$, the error drops by a factor of $2^2 = \mathbf{4\times}$!

```
     Forward:  f(x) ---------> f(x+h)         (O(h) error)
     Backward: f(x-h) <------- f(x)           (O(h) error)
     Central:  f(x-h) <====== f(x) ======> f(x+h)   (O(h^2) error - symmetric!)
```

### 4. The Second Derivative Stencil
Add the two Taylor expansions together:

$$f(x+h) + f(x-h) = 2 f(x) + f''(x) h^2 + \frac{f^{(4)}(x)}{12} h^4 + \dots$$

Subtract $2f(x)$ and divide by $h^2$:

$$D^2 f(x) = \frac{f(x+h) - 2f(x) + f(x-h)}{h^2} = f''(x) + \frac{f^{(4)}(x)}{12} h^2 + \mathcal{O}(h^4)$$

This second-order stencil computes acceleration directly from three spatial samples with $\mathcal{O}(h^2)$ precision!

---

## 11.3 The Catastrophic Cancellation Dilemma (The U-Curve)

In mathematical theory, as $h \to 0$, the finite difference quotient converges toward the exact derivative:

$$\lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x+h) - f(x-h)}{2h} = f'(x)$$

In a physical computer using IEEE 754 floating-point numbers (standard 64-bit `Float64` or `double`), this mathematical limit **fails catastrophically**.

### The Two Competing Errors
When computing derivatives on a digital computer, total error is the sum of two opposing phenomena:

1. **Truncation Error (Mathematical):** The error caused by chopping off higher-order Taylor terms.
   For central difference: $E_{\text{trunc}} \approx \frac{M}{6} h^2$. As $h \to 0$, truncation error decreases.
2. **Roundoff Error (Floating-Point Hardware):** A 64-bit float has 53 bits of mantissa, providing roughly 15 to 17 decimal digits of precision ($\epsilon_{\text{mach}} \approx 2.22 \times 10^{-16}$).
   When $h$ is very small, $f(x+h)$ and $f(x-h)$ are nearly identical numbers. When two numbers sharing the first 14 digits are subtracted, the leading digits cancel to zero, leaving only random rounding noise. This is **catastrophic cancellation**.
   Dividing this tiny rounding noise by a microscopic $h$ amplifies the error:
   $$E_{\text{roundoff}} \approx \frac{2 \epsilon_{\text{mach}} |f(x)|}{2h} = \frac{\epsilon_{\text{mach}} |f(x)|}{h}$$

### Total Computational Error
$$E_{\text{total}}(h) = \frac{M}{6} h^2 + \frac{\epsilon_{\text{mach}}}{h}$$

Look at the dependency on $h$:
- If $h$ is too large ($h = 0.5$): Truncation error dominates ($\approx h^2$).
- If $h$ is too small ($h = 10^{-15}$): Roundoff error explodes ($\approx \frac{10^{-16}}{10^{-15}} = 0.1 \implies 10\%$ error!).
- If $h = 10^{-18}$: $f(x+h)$ rounds to exactly $f(x-h)$, giving $\frac{0}{10^{-18}} = 0$ (100% error!).

```
      Total Error
       E ^
         | \                                 /  Truncation Error ~ h^2
         |  \                               /
         |   \                             /
         |    \     Optimal Step h*       /
         |     \           |             /
         |      \          v            /
         |       '._       .          .'
         |          '-..._____...-''
         |       Roundoff Error ~ 1/h
         +---------------------------------------------> Step Size h
                 10^-16    10^-5        10^-1
```

### Finding the Optimal Step Size $h^*$
Differentiate $E_{\text{total}}(h)$ with respect to $h$ and set to zero:

$$\frac{dE}{dh} = \frac{M}{3} h - \frac{\epsilon_{\text{mach}}}{h^2} = 0 \implies \frac{M}{3} h = \frac{\epsilon_{\text{mach}}}{h^2} \implies h^3 = \frac{3 \epsilon_{\text{mach}}}{M}$$

$$h_{\text{opt}} \approx \left(\epsilon_{\text{mach}}\right)^{1/3} \approx \left(10^{-16}\right)^{1/3} \approx 10^{-5.3} \approx \mathbf{10^{-5}}$$

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **The Golden Rule of Numerical Differentiation:**
> - For **Central Differences** ($\mathcal{O}(h^2)$): Choose $h \approx \sqrt[3]{\epsilon_{\text{mach}}} \approx \mathbf{10^{-5}}$ (yields $\approx 10$ to $11$ digits of precision).
> - For **Forward Differences** ($\mathcal{O}(h)$): Choose $h \approx \sqrt{\epsilon_{\text{mach}}} \approx \mathbf{10^{-8}}$ (yields $\approx 7$ to $8$ digits of precision).
> **Never** choose $h = 10^{-16}$ in floating-point code!

---

## 11.4 Richardson Extrapolation: Boosting to $\mathcal{O}(h^4)$

Can we achieve higher precision without shrinking $h$ into roundoff noise? Yes, through **Richardson Extrapolation**.

Let $D(h)$ denote the central difference computed with step $h$:

$$D(h) = f'(x) + c_1 h^2 + c_2 h^4 + \dots$$

Now compute the central difference with half the step size, $h/2$:

$$D\left(\frac{h}{2}\right) = f'(x) + c_1 \left(\frac{h}{2}\right)^2 + c_2 \left(\frac{h}{2}\right)^4 + \dots = f'(x) + \frac{c_1}{4} h^2 + \frac{c_2}{16} h^4 + \dots$$

Multiply $D(h/2)$ by $4$ and subtract $D(h)$:

$$4 D\left(\frac{h}{2}\right) - D(h) = 4 f'(x) - f'(x) + (c_1 h^2 - c_1 h^2) + \left(\frac{c_2}{4} - c_2\right) h^4 + \dots$$
$$4 D\left(\frac{h}{2}\right) - D(h) = 3 f'(x) - \frac{3}{4} c_2 h^4 + \dots$$

Divide by $3$:

$$f_{\text{extrap}}'(x) = \frac{4 D(h/2) - D(h)}{3} = f'(x) + \mathcal{O}(h^4)$$

By combining two evaluations at moderate step sizes ($h$ and $h/2$), the $h^2$ error term is completely annihilated, producing a **fourth-order accurate** estimate ($\mathcal{O}(h^4)$) completely immune to roundoff noise!

---

## 11.5 The Newton-Raphson Root-Finding Engine

Differentiation is not just for finding rates; it is the most powerful tool for solving non-linear equations $f(x) = 0$.

Suppose we have an estimate $x_n$ near a root. We replace the curve $y = f(x)$ with its tangent line at $(x_n, f(x_n))$:

$$y - f(x_n) = f'(x_n)(x - x_n)$$

The tangent line intersects the horizontal axis ($y = 0$) at a new, vastly superior estimate $x_{n+1}$:

$$0 - f(x_n) = f'(x_n)(x_{n+1} - x_n)$$
$$-f(x_n) = f'(x_n)(x_{n+1} - x_n) \implies x_{n+1} - x_n = -\frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$$

$$\mathbf{x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}}$$

```
       y ^                                .--* (x_0, f(x_0))
         |                             .-'  /
         |                          .-'    / Tangent Line
         |                       .-'      /
         |     Exact Root r   .-'        /
       --+------------*------+----------*----------> x
         |          .'      x_1        x_0
         |       .-'
```

### Quadratic Convergence
When near a simple root ($f'(r) \neq 0$), Newton-Raphson possesses **quadratic convergence**:

$$|x_{n+1} - r| \le C |x_n - r|^2$$

Each iteration **doubles the number of correct decimal digits**!
- Iteration 1: 2 correct digits
- Iteration 2: 4 correct digits
- Iteration 3: 8 correct digits
- Iteration 4: 16 correct digits (full machine precision in 4 steps!)

---

## 11.6 Ten Progressive Worked Numerical Examples

### Example 11.1: Stencil Comparison on a Cubic Polynomial
**Problem:** For $f(x) = x^3 - 4x + 2$, compute the exact derivative at $x = 2$, and compare against Forward, Backward, and Central Difference approximations using $h = 0.1$.

**Step 1:** Exact derivative:
$$f'(x) = 3x^2 - 4 \implies f'(2) = 3(2^2) - 4 = 12 - 4 = 8.000000$$

**Step 2:** Sample function values:
- $f(2) = 2^3 - 4(2) + 2 = 8 - 8 + 2 = 2.000000$
- $f(2 + 0.1) = f(2.1) = (2.1)^3 - 4(2.1) + 2 = 9.261 - 8.4 + 2 = 2.861000$
- $f(2 - 0.1) = f(1.9) = (1.9)^3 - 4(1.9) + 2 = 6.859 - 7.6 + 2 = 1.259000$

**Step 3:** Evaluate stencils:
1. **Forward Difference:**
   $$D_+ = \frac{f(2.1) - f(2)}{0.1} = \frac{2.861 - 2.0}{0.1} = \frac{0.861}{0.1} = 8.610000$$
   $$\text{Absolute Error} = |8.61 - 8.0| = 0.610000$$
2. **Backward Difference:**
   $$D_- = \frac{f(2) - f(1.9)}{0.1} = \frac{2.0 - 1.259}{0.1} = \frac{0.741}{0.1} = 7.410000$$
   $$\text{Absolute Error} = |7.41 - 8.0| = 0.590000$$
3. **Central Difference:**
   $$D_0 = \frac{f(2.1) - f(1.9)}{2(0.1)} = \frac{2.861 - 1.259}{0.2} = \frac{1.602}{0.2} = 8.010000$$
   $$\text{Absolute Error} = |8.01 - 8.0| = \mathbf{0.010000}$$

**Conclusion:** The Central Difference error ($0.01$) is over **$60\times$ smaller** than the one-sided stencils ($0.61$) for the exact same step size!

---

### Example 11.2: Convergence Rate Verification via Step Halving
**Problem:** Using the function from Example 11.1, recalculate the Central Difference with $h = 0.05$ (halving the step size) and verify the $\mathcal{O}(h^2)$ quadratic error reduction.

**Step 1:** Sample function values at $h = 0.05$:
- $f(2 + 0.05) = f(2.05) = (2.05)^3 - 4(2.05) + 2 = 8.615125 - 8.2 + 2 = 2.415125$
- $f(2 - 0.05) = f(1.95) = (1.95)^3 - 4(1.95) + 2 = 7.414875 - 7.8 + 2 = 1.614875$

**Step 2:** Central Difference with $h = 0.05$:
$$D_0(0.05) = \frac{2.415125 - 1.614875}{2(0.05)} = \frac{0.800250}{0.10} = 8.002500$$

**Step 3:** Error comparison:
- Error at $h = 0.10$: $E(0.10) = 8.010000 - 8.0 = 0.010000$
- Error at $h = 0.05$: $E(0.05) = 8.002500 - 8.0 = 0.002500$
- Error ratio:
  $$\frac{E(0.10)}{E(0.05)} = \frac{0.010000}{0.002500} = \mathbf{4.0000}$$

**Conclusion:** Halving $h$ reduced error by exactly $4\times = 2^2$, proving second-order convergence $\mathcal{O}(h^2)$.

---

### Example 11.3: Demonstrating Catastrophic Cancellation in Floating-Point
**Problem:** Estimate the derivative of $f(x) = \sin x$ at $x = 1.0$ using forward difference with step sizes ranging from $h = 10^{-2}$ down to $h = 10^{-16}$. Trace the error curve.

Exact derivative: $f'(1.0) = \cos(1.0) \approx 0.5403023058681397$.

| Step Size $h$ | Forward Difference $D_+(h)$ | Absolute Error $|D_+ - \cos(1)|$ | Primary Error Cause |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :--- |
| $10^{-2}$ | $0.5360859812$ | $4.22 \times 10^{-3}$ | Truncation ($\approx \frac{1}{2}f''(x) h$) |
| $10^{-4}$ | $0.5402602334$ | $4.21 \times 10^{-5}$ | Truncation dominates |
| $10^{-6}$ | $0.5403018851$ | $4.21 \times 10^{-7}$ | Truncation dominates |
| $\mathbf{10^{-8}}$ | $\mathbf{0.5403023053}$ | $\mathbf{5.68 \times 10^{-10}}$ | **OPTIMAL REGION (Minimum Error)** |
| $10^{-10}$ | $0.5403023389$ | $3.30 \times 10^{-8}$ | Roundoff noise begins rising |
| $10^{-12}$ | $0.5403025609$ | $2.55 \times 10^{-7}$ | Roundoff noise growing |
| $10^{-14}$ | $0.5406788555$ | $3.76 \times 10^{-4}$ | Significant digit loss |
| $10^{-16}$ | $0.0000000000$ | $0.540302$ | **100% CATASTROPHIC CANCELLATION** ($f(x+h) == f(x)$) |

This empirical table verifies the theoretical U-curve: pushing $h$ below $10^{-8}$ for forward difference drastically degrades accuracy!

---

### Example 11.4: Second-Order Difference Stencil for Curvature
**Problem:** For $f(x) = \cos x$, compute the exact second derivative at $x = \frac{\pi}{3}$, and compare with the numerical second derivative stencil $D^2(h)$ with $h = 0.1$.

**Step 1:** Exact value:
$$f''(x) = -\cos x \implies f''\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = -\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = -0.5000000$$

**Step 2:** Sample values at $x_0 = \frac{\pi}{3} \approx 1.04719755$:
- $f(x_0) = \cos(1.04719755) = 0.50000000$
- $f(x_0 + 0.1) = \cos(1.14719755) \approx 0.41103390$
- $f(x_0 - 0.1) = \cos(0.94719755) \approx 0.58416801$

**Step 3:** Apply the stencil:
$$D^2(0.1) = \frac{f(x_0 + h) - 2f(x_0) + f(x_0 - h)}{h^2}$$
$$\text{Numerator} = 0.41103390 - 2(0.50000000) + 0.58416801 = 0.41103390 - 1.00000000 + 0.58416801 = -0.00479809$$
$$\text{Denominator} = (0.1)^2 = 0.01$$
$$D^2(0.1) = \frac{-0.00479809}{0.01} = -0.479809$$

**Step 4:** Compare error:
$$\text{Error} = |-0.479809 - (-0.500000)| = 0.020191 \approx 4\%$$

---

### Example 11.5: Applying Richardson Extrapolation
**Problem:** Using the data from Examples 11.1 and 11.2 ($D_0(0.10) = 8.010000$ and $D_0(0.05) = 8.002500$ for $f'(2) = 8.0$), apply Richardson Extrapolation to eliminate the $h^2$ error.

**Step 1:** Apply formula $f_{\text{extrap}}' = \frac{4 D(h/2) - D(h)}{3}$:
$$f_{\text{extrap}}' = \frac{4(8.002500) - 8.010000}{3} = \frac{32.010000 - 8.010000}{3} = \frac{24.000000}{3} = \mathbf{8.000000}$$

**Conclusion:** The extrapolated result matches the exact analytic derivative $8.000000$ down to the final zero!

---

### Example 11.6: Differentiating a Discrete Telemetry Table
**Problem:** An autonomous drone's altimeter records altitude $z$ at $0.2\text{-second}$ intervals:

| Time $t$ (s) | Altitude $z(t)$ (m) |
| :---: | :---: |
| $1.0$ | $42.10$ |
| $1.2$ | $45.68$ |
| $1.4$ | $50.14$ |
| $1.6$ | $55.48$ |
| $1.8$ | $61.70$ |

Estimate the vertical velocity and vertical acceleration at $t = 1.4\text{ seconds}$.

**Step 1:** Step size is uniform: $h = \Delta t = 0.2\text{ s}$. Target index is $t_2 = 1.4\text{ s}$.

**Step 2:** Central difference for velocity $v(1.4) = z'(1.4)$:
$$v(1.4) \approx \frac{z(1.6) - z(1.2)}{2h} = \frac{55.48 - 45.68}{2(0.2)} = \frac{9.80}{0.40} = 24.50\text{ m/s}$$

**Step 3:** Second derivative stencil for acceleration $a(1.4) = z''(1.4)$:
$$a(1.4) \approx \frac{z(1.6) - 2z(1.4) + z(1.2)}{h^2} = \frac{55.48 - 2(50.14) + 45.68}{(0.2)^2}$$
$$\text{Numerator} = 55.48 - 100.28 + 45.68 = 0.88\text{ m}$$
$$\text{Denominator} = 0.04\text{ s}^2$$
$$a(1.4) \approx \frac{0.88}{0.04} = 22.00\text{ m/s}^2$$

**Conclusion:** At $t = 1.4\text{ s}$, vertical velocity is $24.50\text{ m/s}$ and vertical acceleration is $22.00\text{ m/s}^2$.

---

### Example 11.7: Newton-Raphson Calculation of $\sqrt{7}$
**Problem:** Use the Newton-Raphson method to compute $\sqrt{7}$ to 8 decimal places starting from initial guess $x_0 = 3.0$.

**Step 1:** Formulate as a root-finding problem:
$$x = \sqrt{7} \iff x^2 = 7 \iff f(x) = x^2 - 7 = 0$$

**Step 2:** Compute derivative:
$$f'(x) = 2x$$

**Step 3:** Formulate Newton iteration:
$$x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{x_n^2 - 7}{2x_n} = \frac{2x_n^2 - (x_n^2 - 7)}{2x_n} = \frac{x_n^2 + 7}{2x_n} = \frac{1}{2}\left(x_n + \frac{7}{x_n}\right)$$

**Step 4:** Execute iterations:
- **Iteration 0:** $x_0 = 3.00000000$
- **Iteration 1:**
  $$x_1 = \frac{1}{2}\left(3 + \frac{7}{3}\right) = \frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{16}{3}\right) = \frac{8}{3} \approx 2.66666667$$
- **Iteration 2:**
  $$x_2 = \frac{1}{2}\left(2.66666667 + \frac{7}{2.66666667}\right) = \frac{1}{2}(2.66666667 + 2.62500000) = 2.64583333$$
- **Iteration 3:**
  $$x_3 = \frac{1}{2}\left(2.64583333 + \frac{7}{2.64583333}\right) = \frac{1}{2}(2.64583333 + 2.64566929) = 2.64575131$$
- **Iteration 4:**
  $$x_4 = \frac{1}{2}\left(2.64575131 + \frac{7}{2.64575131}\right) = 2.64575131$$

The value has converged to 8 decimal places in just 4 iterations!
$$\sqrt{7} \approx 2.64575131$$

---

### Example 11.8: Newton-Raphson on a Non-Linear Cubic Polynomial
**Problem:** Solve $x^3 - 2x - 5 = 0$ for its unique real root starting from $x_0 = 2.0$.

**Step 1:** $f(x) = x^3 - 2x - 5 \implies f'(x) = 3x^2 - 2$.
**Step 2:** Newton iteration:
$$x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{x_n^3 - 2x_n - 5}{3x_n^2 - 2}$$

**Step 3:** Step-by-step computation:
- **Iteration 0:** $x_0 = 2.0$.
  $$f(2) = 8 - 4 - 5 = -1, \quad f'(2) = 3(4) - 2 = 10$$
  $$x_1 = 2 - \frac{-1}{10} = 2 + 0.1 = 2.100000$$
- **Iteration 1:** $x_1 = 2.1$.
  $$f(2.1) = (2.1)^3 - 2(2.1) - 5 = 9.261 - 4.2 - 5 = 0.061$$
  $$f'(2.1) = 3(2.1)^2 - 2 = 3(4.41) - 2 = 13.23 - 2 = 11.23$$
  $$x_2 = 2.1 - \frac{0.061}{11.23} = 2.1 - 0.00543188 = 2.094568$$
- **Iteration 2:** $x_2 = 2.094568$.
  $$f(2.094568) = (2.094568)^3 - 2(2.094568) - 5 \approx 0.000185$$
  $$f'(2.094568) = 3(2.094568)^2 - 2 \approx 11.1614$$
  $$x_3 = 2.094568 - \frac{0.000185}{11.1614} = 2.094551$$

**Conclusion:** The root is $x \approx 2.094551$.

---

### Example 11.9: Diagnosing Newton-Raphson Failure Modes
**Problem:** Demonstrate why Newton-Raphson fails to converge on $f(x) = x^{1/3}$ when starting from $x_0 = 1.0$.

**Step 1:** $f(x) = x^{1/3} \implies f'(x) = \frac{1}{3}x^{-2/3}$.
**Step 2:** Formulate the Newton step:
$$x_{n+1} = x_n - \frac{x_n^{1/3}}{\frac{1}{3}x_n^{-2/3}} = x_n - 3 x_n^{\frac{1}{3} - \left(-\frac{2}{3}\right)} = x_n - 3x_n^1 = -2x_n$$

**Step 3:** Trace the iteration sequence starting from $x_0 = 1$:
- $x_0 = 1$
- $x_1 = -2(1) = -2$
- $x_2 = -2(-2) = +4$
- $x_3 = -2(4) = -8$
- $x_4 = +16$
- $x_n = (-2)^n$

**Conclusion:** Instead of converging toward the root at $x = 0$, the iterations explode outward with alternating signs toward $\pm\infty$. Newton-Raphson fails because the derivative approaches infinity as $x \to 0$ ($f'(0)$ does not exist).

---

### Example 11.10: 2D Spatial Gradient Estimation for Autonomous Navigation
**Problem:** A robotic exploration vehicle navigates a potential field landscape modeled by elevation $U(x, y) = 3x^2 + 2y^2 - 4xy$. Using central differences with step $h = 0.01$, estimate the gradient vector $\nabla U = \langle \frac{\partial U}{\partial x}, \frac{\partial U}{\partial y} \rangle$ at $(2, 3)$, and compare with the analytic gradient.

**Step 1:** Analytic gradient:
$$\frac{\partial U}{\partial x} = 6x - 4y \implies \frac{\partial U}{\partial x}(2, 3) = 6(2) - 4(3) = 12 - 12 = 0$$
$$\frac{\partial U}{\partial y} = 4y - 4x \implies \frac{\partial U}{\partial y}(2, 3) = 4(3) - 4(2) = 12 - 8 = 4$$
$$\nabla U_{\text{exact}} = \langle 0, 4 \rangle$$

**Step 2:** Numerical partial difference for $\frac{\partial U}{\partial x}$ at $(2, 3)$ with $h = 0.01$:
- $U(2 + 0.01, 3) = U(2.01, 3) = 3(2.01)^2 + 2(3^2) - 4(2.01)(3) = 12.1203 + 18 - 24.12 = 6.0003$
- $U(2 - 0.01, 3) = U(1.99, 3) = 3(1.99)^2 + 2(3^2) - 4(1.99)(3) = 11.8803 + 18 - 23.88 = 6.0003$
$$\frac{\partial U}{\partial x} \approx \frac{6.0003 - 6.0003}{2(0.01)} = \frac{0.0000}{0.02} = 0.0000$$

**Step 3:** Numerical partial difference for $\frac{\partial U}{\partial y}$ at $(2, 3)$ with $h = 0.01$:
- $U(2, 3 + 0.01) = U(2, 3.01) = 3(2^2) + 2(3.01)^2 - 4(2)(3.01) = 12 + 18.1202 - 24.08 = 6.0402$
- $U(2, 3 - 0.01) = U(2, 2.99) = 3(2^2) + 2(2.99)^2 - 4(2)(2.99) = 12 + 17.8802 - 23.92 = 5.9602$
$$\frac{\partial U}{\partial y} \approx \frac{6.0402 - 5.9602}{2(0.01)} = \frac{0.0800}{0.02} = 4.0000$$

**Conclusion:** The estimated numerical gradient is $\langle 0.0000, 4.0000 \rangle$, matching the analytic gradient perfectly to four decimal places.

---
